Brands booking creators to make their ads are pushing up $116B global influencer market

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Brand spending on creator marketing is set to grow twice as fast as spending on advertising as a whole over the next five years, according to advertising research firm Madison & Wall.

The consultancy estimated that creator spending will rise 12% each year until 2030, versus a 6% increase in ad spending each year between 2025 and 2030. In 2026, the researcher estimates that marketers will spend $116 billion on creator activity.

Driving that growth is ad spend intended to boost or promote creator content to a larger audience — which accounted for 44%, or $52 billion, of global creator marketing spending in 2026 — and brands paying creators to produce advertising assets for use in CTV, display and paid social contexts.

The latter accounted for 12% of global creator spending in 2026, but will rise 16% each year until the end of the decade, making it the fastest-growing segment of the creator economy. Traditional sponsored posts or ambassador relationships accounted for 28% of creator spending.

Brands attempting to reach large audiences on social platforms have begun to look to creators not just as a distribution point, but as a source of creative production that can meet the need for a diverse set of creative assets at a lower price point than traditional agency methods.

Unilever CEO Fernando Fernandez, for example, recently said that the CPG giant’s social content had to be constantly refreshed to prevent it from going stale. “The lifespan of a video today is four days,” he said during the firm’s recent Capital Markets Day. “Content has to be renovated because after four days you don’t have diminishing levels of return, you have negative marginal return.”

“The reason we see brands engaging with this is just because it’s cheaper,” said Luke Stillman, managing director at Madison & Wall. “Everybody is trying to squeeze down all those non-working expenses as much as possible.”

As creator rosters get larger — Unilever now works with around 500,000 — the need to manage such programs has driven spending on specialist agencies, platforms and talent representation. Spending on creator intermediaries (fees for specialist agencies, platforms, or talent representation) accounted for 8% of global creator spending.

Stillman said he expected to see spending on that slice grow further in coming years. “Identifying the right creators, contracting with all those creators, managing non-delivery and legal issues and payment issues are really complex, and so there’s plenty of space for ancillary services there,” he said.

“We’re seeing an increase on budgets year-on-year from our clients,” said Andy Ryan, co-founder at social and influencer agency Komodo. Ryan estimated client spending on creator marketing had increased 20%-30% in 2026.

Olivia Wedderburn, head of influence at Born Social, saw an even bigger jump, estimating that client spending on creator activity had risen 50% compared with last year. “I would anticipate that will only continue to grow quite heavily,” she said.

The U.S. market accounted for 43% of creator spending last year, per Madison & Wall estimates, while the U.K. and Germany are the next-largest, at 5% and 4% respectively. The U.S. will see spending grow 15% this year. India was the fastest growing market, at 20%.

Madison & Wall’s estimate for asset-led spending covered several related practices: whitelisting, fees for user-generated content, content licensing, and fees for producing creative assets.

However brands are commissioning it, creator-led production is on the rise. “More of our brand partners are bringing creators in as the production engine for their paid media, not just their organic social,” said Nicholas Spiro, chief commercial officer at Viral Nation.

While cost is a major factor, as Stillman noted, agency marketers also believe creator-made work can engage audiences more effectively than an ad made for the same environment by a creative director.

“Creator-made assets simply connect best with potential customers on a human level,” said Spiro. “They tend to drive performance better than traditional brand creative, and they can be more measurable for certain objectives than organic creator-only content.”

While creator marketing was once dominated by apparel and beauty brands, healthcare, automotive and finance advertisers now spend on creator activity too.

“Where some sectors like fashion and beauty have been on this for a number of years, and therefore they’re already doing a fantastic job at it… some sectors [were] behind and they’re catching up and are now throwing more at it,” said Ryan.

“An ad can make a claim. A parent can prove it,” said Carmen Graham, CMO at Genexa, a youth-targeted healthcare brand.

“Parent creators can also do what we can’t: show the product in real use, in their home. The messier and less art-directed the medicine cabinet, the more we see ourselves and our customer in the content,” she said.

“Our bet is that this commitment to being parent-first will drive the trust this category requires — especially if you’re switching to a new product to support you in the most dire moments of parenthood.”

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

In Q3 earnings, The Trade Desk’s leadership reveals it is searching for answers.

Japanese holdco appointed Mike Nakamura as entertainment and sport chief, as media revenues slip in most markets.

GTA 6 Review - An Astounding, Impossible New Bar For Video Games

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The most anticipated video game of all time is a triumph on every level that exceeds years of unbelievable expectations. You're not ready for Leonida.

Throughout much of my nearly 100 hours of playtime, reviewing Grand Theft Auto 6 felt like a paradox. Finally getting a chance to see what Rockstar has been cooking for close to a decade is both the most exciting moment of my whole career and the most high-pressure thing I’ve ever been lucky enough to take on. How do you possibly begin to talk about something that had whole studios built just for its NPCs?

GTA 6 is the biggest video game of all time, but we really only knew the basics. Something that dawned on me less than an hour in when it became clear just how much had been held back. Even with the weight of 13 years of anticipation on my shoulders, I’m also well aware that nothing I say about Jason and Lucia’s felonious tour of Leonida will influence the minds of anyone even half interested in it.

The great irony of this herculean task is that, more often than not, Grand Theft Auto 6 left me completely speechless, pointing at the screen and gibbering like an idiot at what Rockstar has accomplished. By every measure, it’s a masterclass of a game that not only lives up to the impossible hype but somehow goes even further by redefining what it means to be a GTA game.

Following the narrative heights of Red Dead Redemption 2 and its beloved Arthur Morgan is an unenviable task, but Grand Theft Auto 6 doesn’t disappoint. It follows Jason and Lucia, two small-time criminals who reunite and are quickly forced to rise through the ranks of Leonida’s underworld after a failed job puts them up against Vice City’s most dangerous forces.

So far, so GTA, but there’s more to the plot than the rags-to-riches setup would have you believe. Unsurprisingly, I can’t tell you much about the overarching story and what sets it in motion, but Jason and Lucia’s involvement has far more emotional and mature stakes than any other game in the series before it. Rockstar takes the pair to places I never would have predicted (including some surprisingly dark moments), and it all lands because of how expertly written and characterised both protagonists are.

Jason is more stoic and guarded than any of the more out-there characters we followed throughout Los Santos, but he’s got a heart of gold and lots of charm underneath the gruff exterior, which mostly comes out when he’s with Lucia. He’s harder to read and takes time to warm up to, but by the end, Jason stands comfortably alongside the series' most memorable protagonists, Niko, CJ, and even Red Dead’s revered gunslingers.

Lucia, on the other hand, is without a doubt the best protagonist in Grand Theft Auto history. She’s fiercely loyal, emotionally mature, witty, and doesn’t take crap from anyone, almost to a fault, as her passion and eagerness to prove herself often land her in trouble. As the series’ first female lead (no, I don’t count the pixel blobs from the first game), there’s a lot of pressure on Lucia, but she easily reaches the lofty bar set by Arthur Morgan.

“We believed in this idea of putting a relationship at the center of the game and trying to make a GTA that was still as funny, crazy, shocking, and fun as the previous games but with added heart. But yeah, the process of making something like this you always question things. As you saw with the 2022 hack, game development isn’t pretty. It can take a long time and a lot of iteration to make good on an idea and to get things working. But I think simply the idea that the relationship would be something that the player needed to work at an earn (or ignore if they want to!) felt like exciting new territory.” - Rupert Humphries, Senior Vice President of Narrative

Of course, as great as Jason and Lucia are on their own merits, they come as a pair in GTA 6 and are together for nearly all of the story’s biggest moments. I had some doubts about the Bonnie and Clyde-style romance, as it's not exactly familiar territory for Rockstar. But after seeing their chemistry and working to give them an unbreakable bond through hand-holding, dates, clubbing, and, of course, doing crime as a pair in my playthrough, I simply can’t imagine the two not being madly in love. Some of my favourite moments in the whole game were simply taking Jason and Lucia on a date, grinning as they flirted, and getting unhealthily invested in their relationship.

It’s not just a win for the story, either, as Jason and Lucia’s cooperative gameplay is a huge step up from GTA 5, with far more options than just swapping, from letting your partner drive you to waypoints and having them cover you in firefights to asking them to fence your stolen goods or hang back as a getaway driver. In GTA 6, having a partner in crime finally means exactly that.

That romance might be the focal point and main standout of GTA 6’s story, but there’s plenty to love about the excellent supporting cast as well. Whether you’re supposed to love them like the cool-as-ice mentor figure Raul or hate them like some characters I won’t dare mention, there’s not a single weak link. Though the writing can occasionally be slightly less biting than previous games, Jason and Lucia’s tale tops GTA 4 as the series’ best narrative.

Perhaps my favourite character of the whole game is Karim, the hacker in Raul’s group who provides some surprising Watch Dogs 2 vibes.

Sony is right - Grand Theft Auto 6 is probably going to play best on PlayStation.

It would have been easy (or at least easier) for Rockstar to take GTA 5’s lucrative formula and just polish the graphics a bit, but one of the biggest strengths of Grand Theft Auto 6 is how it completely rebuilds the main tenets of the whole series. For starters, core mechanics like movement, shooting, and driving all feel far better than ever thanks to extensive improvements and tweaks.

Take GTA’s infamous mash X to sprint controls, for instance, which have been completely reworked for a more standardised and smoother experience. Jason and Lucia are both far more agile than previous protagonists and have more vertical options at every moment, which makes the simple act of getting around on foot much more enjoyable. The only downside to the rework, and spoilers for the rest of this review - the game as a whole - is that the controls can sometimes be a little overcomplicated due to how many interactions you have at your disposal.

It did take me a good few hours to adjust to the new control scheme, but it’ll be incredibly tough to go back now. There is an option for the classic version if you really struggle, though.

“There was lot a lot of internal team feedback to look at our controls and responsiveness after we launched RDR2, so we doubled down on exploring an ‘experimental’ control scheme as it was called internally at the time. The experiment kind of stalled mid development due to other competing priorities but was revitalized by Rob (Head of Development). After that, we all strongly felt it should be the default control scheme as we’d had consistently good feedback from the team and user research. We still offer classic GTA controls in the setting menu for those prefer them. Indeed, we have many people at R* who still do! There are also a lot more options for players to explore in the menu. Hopefully, there is a scheme or set of options for everyone to enjoy the game!” - David Hynd, Vice President of Technology

Shooting, meanwhile, takes cues from Red Dead Redemption 2 and its weightier weapons, which already felt inspired by Max Payne 3’s stellar gunfights. There’s even a Dead Eye/Bullet Time slow-motion focus mode for both protagonists, rather than being locked as a special ability for one character, and it becomes an essential tool as you go up against the gangs and cops of Leonida. It’s the best combat has ever felt in a Grand Theft Auto game by a country mile.

The same goes for driving, which sits somewhere between GTA 5 and GTA 4 thanks to a greater focus on vehicle weight, physics, and damage while somehow still retaining the buttery smooth controls and flow. Driving is also the most immediately obvious example of Rockstar not just improving how Grand Theft Auto 6 plays, but recontextualising every mechanic’s implementation with a greater focus on worldbuilding and player involvement.

Unlike previous games in the series where you could grab a fast car and bomb it down the city streets without so much as tapping the brakes, Leonida’s denser traffic and narrower streets (including a ton of painfully realistic roadworks) mean you need to be paying attention at all times. It’s only when you start to master the new systems and put the effort in that you can weave through traffic like a pro, which makes it infinitely more rewarding. Couple that with cars requiring far more work to attain thanks to higher security and a ridiculous amount of upgrade and modding options, and vehicles become so much more than just something you steal, park in your garage, and don’t give a second thought to.

On the other hand, gunfights are no longer about just aiming for the head with whatever arsenal you’ve got in your back pocket, as you now need to incapacitate enemies with clean, targeted shots to the knees and arms if you want to keep a professional criminal profile. You also have a more limited choice of weapons, forcing you to plan ahead and keep your trunk stocked before a firefight. Even crime, the main thing Grand Theft Auto is known for, is completely different in GTA 6. Not only are the police more advanced than ever before, with multiple stages of realistic awareness of your actions that incentivise masks, stealth, and being as efficient as possible, but there’s far more effort that needs to be put in for even the simplest of sins.

"A lot of work, yeah! There were a few key areas we needed to change for cops in particular on this game. The perception/recognition system needed a complete overhaul. On V/RDR the cops always knew where the player was located, but with the new model they now rely on proper search/investigation routines, guided by witness reports and evidence (e.g. dead bodies, abandoned vehicles). K9 units can sense the player too, so you need to keep on your toes when they are around! And, of course, we added appearance and outfit recognition to add depth/roleplay mechanics to cop evasion. Having an unknown wanted state created lots of great gameplay moments, where you could nonchalantly leave a crime scene undetected. And adding in Heat added a level of persistence that was lacking in previous games. The wanted UI also took a lot of time to finesse/balance. We added threat indicators to give the player more feedback around how they were being perceived by cops/enemies in game. This opened up some cool stealth gameplay opportunities, but was also crucial seeing as we removed enemies from the radar. Once you are spotted, we now rely on cops calling it in before you fully known. This gives the player a window of opportunity to deal with the situation before things kick off.  But yeah, it’s been a massive effort. Fueled by constant tweaks and iteration over many years." - David Hynd, Vice President of Technology

Gone are the days of completing a few story missions and having millions in the bank without anything to spend it on, as money is incredibly hard to come by in Vice City. You really need to work for it, whether through simple but low-reward gas station stick-ups and jewel store heists, or storming gang hideouts for drugs you can sell on to fences. What’s most impressive about all of the crime opportunities is that they’re completely emergent and up to the player, a massive leap forward from Los Santos story-specific heists and very basic convenience store shakedowns.

As if being able to pull off story mission-esque heists at any moment wasn’t enough, GTA 6 has also made some notable improvements to the main campaign. When they’re not trying to make Jason and Lucia feel like real people with quieter moments, story missions are energetic, bombastic, and, unlike some criticisms of Red Dead Redemption 2, give the player more freedom and control than before. While I’m sure some players will prefer the classic, more thoughtless approach, for my money Grand Theft Auto 6 is a better and more considered game on every single front, pushing the series formula to impossible new heights.

In a game that’s all triumphs, the biggest of all is Grand Theft Auto 6’s meticulously crafted world, which is so unbelievably impressive it single-handedly justifies the long wait. From the murky swamps near Leonida Keys to the neon-lit upper echelons of Capri, Leonida is jaw-dropping at every turn (fittingly for Vice City, especially at night) and a downright feat of engineering to get it all working without any issues on PS5 Pro. Some things, especially the weather system and frankly ridiculous lighting, need to be seen to be believed.

I’ve never stopped to smell the roses so much in a game, but it’s impossible not to soak in all of the work and love that’s gone into every explorable interior, every beach packed with NPCs you can interact with or just hear have detailed conversations, every neighbourhood with potential things to steal, and every flea market with functioning shops. Almost all of the effort feels necessary to deliver on the promise of Leonida, but you can often feel Rockstar flexing its penchant for the little details (such as food being realistically eaten and the unique games that are playable on the PlayStation parody) that make these worlds feel so real.

Grand Theft Auto 6 also easily has the best radio selection the series has seen since Vice City. Justice’s original soundtrack is also incredible and punctuates major story moments perfectly.

Rockstar’s foray into heightened realism in Red Dead Redemption 2 has clearly left its mark on GTA 6, which focuses heavily on immersion and realism. You can shower, work out at the gym to build muscle, eat food to put on weight, get tired from not sleeping enough, need to keep vehicles filled with fuel, and have more hairstyle and clothing options than you know what to do with. I was a little worried it’d go too far and end up a little intense like RDR2, but Rockstar has taken the right amount of inspiration from that game while still making sure GTA isn’t getting too close to a life-sim.

“I personally love going into a gas station, buying a hot dog and a slushie and walking out alternating between eating and drinking and walking down the street doing that, then throwing the empty cup in the trash. I love getting a six pack of beers and walking along drinking them one at a time, throwing them around!”- Rob Nelson, Head of Development

Beyond being so detailed and immersive, Leonida is also just an incredibly varied and handcrafted county that feels unique no matter where you go. It’s a far cry from GTA 5’s handful of city areas mixed with vast wilderness that ends up getting ignored, and it helps that it’s stuffed with side missions, minigames, and emergent events that keep the water-cooler spirit Rockstar’s games are so well known for.

It might feel like I’ve said a lot about GTA 6 (it certainly does to me, anyway), but even with close to 100 hours, there are still whole areas I’ve barely explored, mechanics I’ve only glanced at, easter eggs to find, and activities that are begging for more of my time - it’s an absolute juggernaut. There’s also so much I can’t talk about or simply don’t want to get into to keep the sense of discovery for everyone else.

Without a shadow of a doubt, Grand Theft Auto 6 is the best open-world game I’ve ever played, and one of the best games full stop, with only incredibly minute nitpicks barely worth mentioning that do nothing to drag it down from masterpiece status. Its jaw-droppingly detailed world, completely revamped and recontextualised mechanics, and heartfelt narrative with two stellar leads put it far above the rest of the incredible series and set an impossible new bar for the medium as a whole. I'm sure we’ll never see another game with such unreal hype that actually lives up to and exceeds expectations, but you can rest easy knowing that the long road to Leonida was worth walking.

We finally got a chance to play The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake and it's still one of the best games ever made.

Hating Turkey Doesn’t Make You Cool

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It is fun to be contrarian. I admit to the light thrill it gives me to tell people that I hate oat milk, or that I prefer a wet martini. It’s healthy for all of us to have our preferences. But sometimes, what should be a matter of personal taste goes viral, amassing an army of haters who make this preference their entire personality. Think back to the great cake vs pie wars of the 2010s, when anyone who admitted to liking cake was a tasteless rube. This isn’t even about what you like anymore—it’s about the attention that comes with going against the mainstream.

This is all to say that complaining about turkey on Thanksgiving has got to stop. It is good, and I need people to stop pretending as if it’s not.

You would think, from the way people talk about turkey around Thanksgiving, that a flock of the birds came into their house and killed their mother. In 2020, Business Insider wrote that turkey isn’t good and you should leave it off your Thanksgiving table. Vox insists the (horrifying) numbers of turkeys wasted every year are in service of “a dry, flavorless bird no one likes,” while Milk Street claims anyone who likes it must be “pretending.” Even this very publication called it “The Big Freaky Chicken.”

Respectfully, what on earth are you people talking about? Every year, I cannot wait for the unveiling of the turkey. It is the ultimate reminder that this isn’t a normal meal because we do not eat turkey on the regular.

Over the years, my family has more often entrusted me with the cooking. We tend to do a version of this Martha Stewart en papillote method and I relish in the work. There is care required to ensure the meat is juicy and golden, and the resulting gravy silky and rich. There is great satisfaction in carrying out the gorgeous centerpiece. There is a subtle butteriness to the meat, and something wilder that even the most factory farmed bird can’t erase. Roasting a chicken is for a Tuesday. Roasting a turkey is for when you have the day off work.

Part of the joy of the turkey, of course, is the leftovers. Turkey congee, turkey pot pie, turkey melts, which will only exist because of Thanksgiving. A feast stretched out, reminding you that abundance does not last for just a day. Do not rob yourself of such plenty.

Obviously, the haters have not stopped millions of turkeys from being served every Thanksgiving. The biggest complaint seems to be that the turkey is never cooked well. And I’ll give you that, with a bigger bird, that you only encounter once a year, it takes planning and effort to ensure the middle doesn’t become a powdery mess while the legs are still raw. But I don’t know, try. There are many things that are tricky to cook that are beloved precisely because of the exhilaration of the challenge, so I don’t know why turkey gets all the heat.

If you’re here, it means you like to cook, even love it, and there are no shortage of recipes guiding you toward bird nirvana. So put your hobby to good use, get a meat thermometer, and figure it out. Because as much fun as it is to be a hater, it’s even more fun to prove everyone wrong with a feast.

Rents Edge Up for First Time Since June 2023 as Construction Boom Fades: Realtor.com® October Rent Report

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Median asking rent rises $1 year over year to $1,692 as third-quarter multifamily completions fall 26%

AUSTIN, Texas, Nov. 18, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Rents edged higher in October for the first time in more than three years as the wave of new multifamily housing that helped bring rents down recedes. According to the Realtor.comÂ® October 2026 Rent Report, the median asking rent for studio, one- and two-bedroom properties across the 50 largest U.S. metropolitan areas rose $1, or 0.1%, year over year to $1,692 per month.

The modest increase marks the first year-over-year rent growth since June 2023, but monthly rents remain $82, or 4.6%, below their August 2022 peak and $221, or 15%, above October 2019 levels.

"Renters still face a very different affordability picture than they did before the pandemic," said Jiayi Xu, senior economist at Realtor.comÂ®. "Elevated borrowing costs may keep more households renting, just as new deliveries slow. That makes the local construction pipeline increasingly important to the outlook for rent relief."

Larger units led the shift. Two-bedroom rents rose 0.3% year over year to $1,889, their first annual increase since May 2023, while one-bedroom rents increased 0.1% to $1,566, their first gain since June 2023. Studio rents fell 1.2% to $1,411, marking their 39th consecutive month of annual declines.

Construction Boom Fades, Limiting Further Rent ReliefNearly 1.5 million units in buildings with five or more units were completed between 2023 and 2025, 46.1% more than in between 2017 to 2019. That surge helped hold rents down, but deliveries have now returned to near pre-pandemic levels.

In the third quarter of 2026, multifamily completions fell 26% year over year to a seasonally adjusted annual rate of 330,000 units, just 2.8% above the corresponding 2017 to 2019 average. Units under construction declined 2.7% to 674,000, while starts fell 9.2% to an annualized pace of 403,000 units. Higher financing and construction costs have likely contributed to the slowdown in starts.

With fewer units under construction, deliveries are unlikely to rebound over the next 12 months. The direction of rents will depend largely on whether new supply can outpace rental demand.

Multifamily Deliveries Normalize as the Construction Boom Unwinds

Note: Under Construction is tracked as a seasonally adjusted level. Starts and Completions are tracked as a seasonally adjusted annual rate.

Regional Pipelines Point to Different Rent OutlooksCompletions fell year over year in all four U.S. regions, but the supply outlook varies.

The Northeast recorded the steepest drop in completions, down 49.3%, and was the only region with deliveries below its pre-pandemic baseline. Starts rebounded sharply, but are unlikely to lift deliveries in 2027. Thinner supply could add upward pressure in markets such as Philadelphia and Pittsburgh, where rents have already returned from decline to growth (0.6% and 1.5% year-over-year, respectively).

The Midwest has the largest pipeline relative to pre-pandemic levels, with units under construction 33.3% above that baseline. Starts eased from a year-earlier peak but remained elevated. Despite the Midwest's relatively large construction pipeline, rents are already rising in several markets, including Chicago and St. Louis, both up 3.2% year over year, and Minneapolis, up 2.0%.

In the South, completions and starts were just 3.7% above pre-pandemic levels, although the construction pipeline remained elevated. Rent declines are already narrowing in some markets. In Austin, Texas, the annual decline slowed to 0.9% from 7.0% in October 2025, while Oklahoma City posted its first annual increase since January 2025, rising 1.2%.

The West was the only region with starts below pre-pandemic levels, down 15%, raising the risk of tighter supply ahead. Renters in Los Angeles, Denver and Phoenix continued to see annual declines, but weaker construction activity could limit that relief in the years ahead.

Rental units include apartments as well as private rentals (condos, townhomes, single-family homes). We use rental sources that reliably report data each month within the 50 largest metropolitan areas. Realtor.comÂ® began publishing regular monthly rental trends reports in October 2020, with data history extending to March 2019.

28 Seats: European Carrier Reveals First-Ever Premium Economy Cabin For US Routes

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Irish flag carrier Aer Lingus has become the latest airline to join the premium economy revolution, with the airline having just unveiled its new product offering ahead of its launch next October. It plans to fit 28 spacious recliner seats on ten of its Airbus A330 widebody twinjets. This will represent the bulk of its twin-aisle fleet, with ch-aviation showing that it currently has two Airbus A330-200s and 12 A330-300s.

While the introduction of premium economy for the first time in the Irish flag carrier's history is the main headline, Aer Lingus will also use this refurbishment as an opportunity for a nose-to-tail reimagining of its A330 interiors, with business and economy class also changing. Passengers flying on Aer Lingus' US routes will be the first to benefit from the update, with Dublin to New York JFK being the first route.

Aer Lingus has chosen the RECARO R4 seat for its new premium economy cabin on the Airbus A330, which will be able to accommodate 28 passengers in four seven-abreast (2-3-2) rows. These flyers will be able to look forward to an increased seat pitch of 38 inches, with the seats measuring 18-19 inches in width. They will also offer guests a greater deal of recline, at eight inches. The soft product is strong too.

On this front, Aer Lingus says that its premium economy passengers can expect, among other things, "an upgraded three-course dining experience, complimentary drinks throughout the flight, comfortable bedding, an amenity kit and noise-reducing headphones." All the while, Starlink WiFi will help them to stay more connected inflight than ever before. Lynne Embleton, the CEO of Aer Lingus, said that:

"Our ambition here is simple. We want to give our customers greater choice, comfort, and connectivity, while creating an experience that feels unmistakably Aer Lingus."

It is no secret in the world of modern commercial aviation that the transatlantic market between Europe and North America is one of the most lucrative corridors around. As such, premium demand is often high, and Aer Lingus is set to tap into this trend by launching its newly fitted A330s on the long-haul route from Dublin (DUB) to New York (JFK). These flights will begin in just under a year, on October 1, 2027.

Initially, Aer Lingus plans to deploy its premium economy-equipped A330s on the EI104/105 rotation. These flights are operated today by the A330-300, which is the larger and more numerous of Aer Lingus' two variants of the Airbus A330. At the time of writing, these widebody twinjet aircraft feature a two-class configuration with 30 business class flatbeds and either 283 or 287 economy class seats on board.

As more and more of Aer Lingus' Airbus A330s are retrofitted with the new cabins, the carrier will be able to roll the product out on a growing variety of, as it puts it, "additional North American routes." According to Cirium, an aviation analytics company, its present A330 services across the pond from Dublin serve Boston, Chicago, Las Vegas, Los Angeles, Miami, New York, Orlando, and San Francisco.

The 8-hour flight rule explains when Premium Economy beats Business Class on price, comfort, sleep, and corporate travel value.

With ten of Aer Lingus' A330s being taken out of service for the premium economy refit, this presents an opportunity for the Irish flag carrier to undertake a nose-to-tail reimagining of its widebody twinjet fleet. This is exactly what it plans to do, hence the aforementioned addition of Starlink WiFi. This will also see it refit its business class cabins with 28 Collins Elevation suites in a four-abreast 1-2-1 layout.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

This setup will provide every passenger with direct aisle access, and, when converted to a bed, Aer Lingus says that these seats stretch to a length of 78 inches (1.98 meters). The carrier is also reimagining its business class catering offering and fitting its A330s with a snack bar. As for economy class, Aer Lingus promises "ergonomic seating, six-way adjustable leather headrests, [and] refreshed dining options."

Record crowds, legends - Bordeaux bid to escape amateur game

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Bordeaux fans protested when the club were demoted to the sixth tier

The latest stop on Zinedine Zidane's homecoming tour was an important one for the new France head coach. When Les Bleus took on Turkey in Bordeaux on Sunday night, the ex-midfielder was returning to the city in which he spent four formative years of his playing career, between 1992 and 1996.

"I became a man in Bordeaux," Zidane fondly recalled in the build-up. France edged triumphed 2-1 with an uninspiring display, but it did not dampen the mood of the 40,000 fans inside the Stade Atlantique.

While the 1998 World Cup winner was not necessarily in familiar surroundings (the out-of-town stadium was built for Euro 2016, 20 years after his departure for Juventus), locals made every effort to make him feel at home, holding up a commemorative tifo before kick-off and chanting Zizou's name throughout.

Just a few miles up the road earlier that afternoon, Zidane's former club were playing their fifth match of the season in the sixth-tier Regional 1, in a north-western suburb of the city.

Thousands of Bordeaux fans had made the short away outing to Saint-Medard-en-Jalles' 3000-seater ground to watch their team seal a fifth win in five this season.

Girondins de Bordeaux, the six-time French champions, who were once regulars in European cups, now find themselves facing amateur opposition on a weekly basis.

Since 2022, the club has plummeted from Ligue 1 to the sixth tier through a combination of on-pitch struggles, mismanagement at all levels, and sanctions due to their financial troubles.

Listen to the latest Football Daily podcast

The club was taken over in 2021 by Gerard Lopez, who was previously in charge of Formula 1's Lotus as well as another French side, Lille.

While Bordeaux were already hamstrung by debts at that point, the situation on and off the pitch worsened under the Spanish-Luxembourgish businessman as the men's first team finished rock bottom of Ligue 1 in his first season.

A couple of seasons in Ligue 2 followed, before the club was docked down to the fourth tier in 2024. In their first National 2 season, they notably signed Andy Carroll but ultimately missed out on promotion.

The following campaign saw a repeat of that near miss, and the club was expelled from national-level football altogether this summer over their finances.

With Bordeaux left on the brink and whittled down to just eight full-time employees (down from around 250 a few years beforehand), Lopez sold up to Park Bench, a group of mainly British USA-based investors who also own Scottish side Dunfermline as well as a minority stake in Spanish second tier side Cordoba.

The supporters turned out in their numbers for the first home match of the campaign, which had been delayed amid the uncertainty over whether the club could continue running, against Stade Montois.

More than 11,000 tickets sold, a record crowd for that level of French football.

"140 years of history, we won't be stopped," read one of the banners held up by the Ultramarines fan group, who also made it a point to thank the seven first-team players who had stayed despite the club's double relegation.

The team is centred around a core of players with Ligue 1 and Ligue 2 experience. Steve Shamal, who scored a hat-trick in that 4-1 victory over Stade Montois, previously turned out for the likes of Caen and Annecy in the second tier.

"We know we should be higher up, but that certainty can also be a trap," said Shamal after the game. "We shouldn't think that it's going to be easy.

"We need to respect our opponents if we're going to put in good performances."

As the sixth tier of French football is regionalised, only the league winner in the Nouvelle Aquitaine region's top flight will earn promotion.

Their on-pitch success so far this season comes as little surprise given the gulf in quality compared to the rest of the league. Over the summer, Bordeaux notably managed to hold on to Europa League-winning full-back Almamy Toure, while former Belgian youth international striker Baptiste Guillaume also joined.

Several former players have also looked to get involved in Bordeaux's revival.

Benoit Tremoulinas, the former French international who was a part of the title-winning squad in 2008-09, notably announced that at the age of 40 he would be lacing up his boots again seven years after injury forced him to retire.

Paul Baysse, the former centre-back, is an assistant to head coach Arnaud Cormier.

Benoit Tremoulinas spent six years at Bordeaux, winning a league title and two cups, before also twice winning the Europa League with Sevilla

Perhaps the most significant endorsement the new owners could have hoped for was that of Alain Giresse, widely regarded as the club's greatest player. The Euro 1984 winner, who won three league titles while playing more than 500 times for the Marine et Blanc, was in attendance for the match against Stade Montois. He took the ceremonial kick-off before giving a full-time dressing room speech.

The new management unveiled their long-term plans for the club on Monday at Le Haillan, the city-owned training ground which includes a chateau that hosts the club's headquarters.

"We are humbled to have this responsibility," Park Bench investor Jeremy Green said.

"The club doesn't belong to Park Bench, it belongs to Bordeaux, it belongs to the 11,000 people who came to watch a Regional 1 game, to the players and to the community. We have a responsibility to rebuild this club.

"Our commitment to the community and to the players is that we will be fiscally responsible and patient.

"We're not a financial institution, we're a collection of individuals who bring with them business, sporting, analytical, and entrepreneurship skills."

Dominique Delport, the club's new president, revealed he knew Green and Twining through his wife, who studied at Oxford with them.

"We can learn from the club's golden years," the former journalist and advertising executive told BBC Sport, before adding that he would look to bring club legends to every league game.

Bordeaux won the Coupe de France as recently as 2013

While the club was sold for a symbolic euro, it did involve taking on long-standing debts which spanned transfer fees, stadium rent, and a compensation claim by former head coach Vladimir Petkovic.

Last Friday, the city's commercial court approved a request to restructure the debt as part of the club's continuation plan.

When Bordeaux were expelled from national-level football by the French FA at the start of the summer, the club used several avenues to appeal while the takeover was being finalised.

"We thought we had acquired the club in National 1 [the fourth tier]," Green and co-investor James Twining, told BBC Sport.

"Initially we walked away, but we were encouraged to come back," Green added. "We established that we were going to be able to restructure things and make it work.

"People around the club and in the local community encouraged us to try to find a way."

The six-time French champions have a long way to go before returning to the top flight.

"We're committing to a sensible financial structure, to a well-run club, and we're talking about 10 years to get this club back to Ligue 1," the new investors added.

A crucial step towards that long-term goal will be returning to the professional leagues, which would allow them to re-open the youth academy they were made to close when they dropped down.

Rebuilding the women's team, who were involved in the Champions League only a few seasons ago, is another one of the new owners' objectives.

Keeping the fans onside throughout the process will also be key, not least after the rift that opened up between groups during the previous ownership.

In any case, if the record-breaking turnout for Bordeaux's return to league action is anything to go by, their support seems unlikely to waver anytime soon.

Swansea City backed for Roberto Martinez or Pablo Hernandez move

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Swansea City have had to take a double-hit in recent weeks, with a dip in form and interest in their manager from elsewhere, but FLW's Swans fan pundit is optimistic over the identity of his replacement.

Swansea City's ascent to the top of the Championship was one of the surprises of the start of the season in the second tier. Seven wins from their first ten games took them to the top of the pile, while other below them struggled to find the consistency required to dislodge them.

That, however, all came to a juddering stop in the month building up to the international break. Four defeats in their last five have dropped them to sixth place in the table, and there was more bad news for them with reports that head coach Vitor Matos is in the sights of Ligue Un club Strasbourg.

With Matos's departure for France imminent, Swansea are in the hunt for a new manager, and with the top half of the Championship table looking extremely tight this season and tricky matches coming up, whoever the new incumbent is will need to get the team back on form to build on their excellent start.

Football League World have been in contact with our Swans fan pundit Will Hughes about the changing of the guard at The Swansea.com Stadium, and in particular about the possibility of the former West Bromwich Albion manager Carlos Corberan coming to his club, and Will believes that his club currently enticing enough to attract that calibre of manager: "I think Corberan would be good. He's certainly a name that should be floating around there."

Will believes that his club is a more attractive option as a result of changes behind the scenes over the last couple of years: "Honestly, I don't know who Matos's replacement is going to be. I'm not sure how big our pull is now, but I'd definitely say that the Swans job is a lot more desirable now managers now than what it was this time last year, for where we are in the league, the outside noise with over goings-on at the club, with the ownership of Modric and Snoop Dogg, and the fact that we've got more finance now. We've got a better squad. It's definitely a more desirable club."

Will is setting his hopes high over the identity of the next Swansea boss, with one former manager being someone he'd love to see back at his club: "This time last year I certainly wouldn't have been saying some of the names that are floating about now. One name that's been floated by Swans fans has been Roberto Martinez. I would love it if he came home, but dare I say maybe he's a bit above us after managing Portugal and Belgium, two very high profile jobs. Would he see this as a step down? Maybe he doesn't want to ruin his legacy here. I can't see it happening, but I'd love it."

And he had another suggestion with a Swansea connection who he feels could be a realistic target for his club: "Pablo Hernandez has been noted. Castellon are in the same bracket as us under Jamestown Analytics, Tony Bloom's company. I don't know whether they have a clause that we can't import another Jamestown club's manager, but going by the outside noise and the statistics that we see from Castellon, he could be a good fit for us."

Will sees the replacement as potentially an opportunity for his club, and is grateful to Matos for his role in improving Swansea's profile: "I don't whether we'd be looking higher. For me, I'd love it if we upgraded on Matos. Matos came in and he did his job. When he came in, Swans were floundering near the bottom. He kept us up and got us to a mid-table finish, which is what we were expecting, and now this year we've had a really good start, he's got us in the play-offs, near the top of the division. Whether he'd have kept us there we'll never know, but he got us to that point so he deserves credit for making the job more desirable."

Who would you like to see as the new Swansea City manager? Let us know in the comments below!

Our Swansea correspondent is also aware of the importance of the new manager stepping straight in and getting the team back on track: "So the question is can we get someone to keep us there or maybe take us a little bit further up. You'd want someone who's going to come in and hit the ground running. I know it's not as easy as that, but you don't want to bring in someone where you're thinking, "Maybe it'll work, maybe it won't." You want to upgrade. I don't know how far our pull is now, with Jamestown on board, whether we can look at the bigger leagues, at clubs and managers we wouldn't have over the last few years. There's a few out of work. You've mentioned Corberan, I've mentioned Roberto Hernandez. Kieran McKenna would be good, but I've heard we're priced out on him."

And he has one particular fixture in mind, which is coming up on the 29th November: "It's typical that the new manager's first game is probably going to be the South Wales derby. Hopefully it'll be another Garry Monk masterclass; 3-0 in his first game. But we'd hope it doesn't go the way it did afterwards towards the end of his tenure, because it turned sour in the end. I keep saying we'll wait and see. I'd an expect an announcement today that Matos has gone, but we've got Leon Brittan and Chris O'Leary, two legends, looking after us for two games and you'd think hopefully we'll get a new manager in next week, and we can go from there."

Will feels that while the Cardiff game is extremely important, Swansea's season won't be defined by his team's matches against these opponents: "All I would say to Swans fans is not to get on the new manager's back if we don't get the result we want against Cardiff. As much as we all want to beat them, it's a marathon not a sprint. We may lose to them but beat them away from home. Let's not jump on the new manager's back, or Leon Brittan or the club if we don't win that game. It's not the end of the world. It may feel like it, believe me. I've got into some stinking moods when we've lost to Cardiff in the past."

And he believes that the best thing that his fellow supporters can do is get fully behind the team, no matter who the opponents: "But whoever's in charge, let's not get on their back. It was different when we had someone like Michael Duff or Luke Williams when they lost their last South Wales derbies because of the situations that were going on, Luke Williams flirting with West Brom and Michael Duff saying what he said about the derby, so that's fair enough. But let's not get on their back, let's get behind them and hopefully start with a win on Saturday. It's a tricky game against West Brom, but forget about the fact that we haven't got a manager or where they are in the league, it's a game we've got to try and win to stop the rot of our poor form."

Whoever it is who ends up filling the shoes of Vitor Matos at The Swansea.com Stadium will have one immediate job; to turn around the form of a team who were flying at the start of the season. Given that Swansea City haven't finished above 10th place in the Championship in the previous five years, few were expecting them to get near the top of the table this time around, but they managed this on the 1st September and stayed there for most of the next six weeks.

The names mentioned in connection with the vacant position all have something to recommend them. Carlos Corberan has Championship experience from spells with Huddersfield Town and West Bromwich Albion. Pablo Hernandez has a previous Swansea connection from having played there from 2012 to 2014. And Kieran McKenna built a strong reputation from taking Ipswich Town from League One to the Premier League.

And whoever the identity of the new Swansea manager might be, he'll have a tough start. West Bromwich Albion are the Swans' next opponents, and they're one place above them in the table. Next after that come Middlesbrough, who are in second place in the table. And then comes the big derby game against Cardiff City.

So the question facing the club is that of which was the blip? Were Swansea City just on an explosively good run of form at the start of the season which is now settling towards where most people would have put them in the summer, or did Vitor Matos build a squad that is capable of going the entire gruelling length of a 46-game season near the top of the table?

Whichever the answer to that question turns out to be, what is undeniable is that Swansea City demonstrated their potential over the opening two months of the season. If the new Swansea manager can capture that form again, the Swans could yet find themselves pushing to end their nine-year exile in the Championship by its end.

The Perfect 'Deadwood' Cast if the Show Were Made in 2026

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It's been more than 20 years, and Deadwood still remains one of the greatest shows in HBO history. Created by David Milch, the three-season series is set in 1876 Deadwood, South Dakota, and follows the titular camp as it operates as a lawless sinkhole of crime, gambling, and rough-and-tumble survival. The town explodes from a muddy, chaotic tent camp into a bustling settlement driven by a massive gold strike that draws prospectors, outlaws, entrepreneurs, and working women.

Filled to the brim with dramatized figures from history, Deadwood is a masterful mix of Western idealism and the true grit of surviving in a changing moment in history. At its core, the series explores how people piece together rules, commerce, and community from pure chaos when formal government and laws are absent. The heart of the show centers on incredible characters, brought to life by an iconic ensemble. So, what would happen if Deadwood were made in 2026? Though the show is untouchable for devoted fans, this fantasy casting honors the project's heartbeat with a new ensemble of actors who capture the characters' essence.

The true heartbeat of Deadwood came from the heroic man with the badge, Seth Bullock. He arrives in the lawless mining camp as a former Montana marshal looking to start a new life. Alongside his friend and business partner, Sol Star (John Hawkes), he sets up a hardware and general store before becoming Deadwood's first sheriff. Loosely based on the real-life historical figure, much of Seth's story revolves around keeping Deadwood morally afloat. Timothy Olyphant brought him to life perfectly, launching him into an archetype that made him famous. Offering a soft-spoken, gentle demeanor, Olyphant subverted the traditional Western hero. With a physical presence and fixed glare, Olyphant's restraint made Seth's moments of explosion feel earned. He masked a deep internal rage, but he was still quite stubborn.

As one half of the dominating duo on Deadwood, selecting an actor who has the same essence as Olyphant is required. A brilliant selection would be Logan Lerman. His career evolution aligns perfectly with Seth's core tension. He went from boyish charm with a quiet side hiding impulses within. In projects like Hunters and Indignation, Lerman mastered the art of playing characters who look unassuming but harbor boiling, dark intensity beneath the surface. He carries a classic, honorable leading man quality that evokes a trustworthiness that would allow him to lead a camp of chaotic and unpredictable characters. Even early in his career, Lerman excelled at characters with heavy dramatic undertones, namely The Perks of Being a Wallflower, but as he has matured, he has built the gravitas to take on a hardened lawman.

There is no doubt that Al Swearengen is one of the greatest television characters of all time. Some might have felt he was a caricature compared to more grounded characters, but Swearengen's quirks and eccentricities made him genuinely Western. Swearengen is a criminal operator, pimp, and master manipulator who runs the most successful bar and brothel in Deadwood. He is foul-mouthed, violent, and cold-blooded, yet he has a complex code of pragmatism, fierce loyalty to his inner circle, and a driving urge to guide Deadwood toward survival. He's a brilliant antihero who begins the series as the established villain, only to be outdone as new faces arrive. Swearengen is a masterclass in character, thanks to Ian McShane's pitch-perfect performance. Though based on the real-life figure, McShane made him larger than life. He blended a terrifying ruthlessness with theatrical, Shakespearean depth. McShane delivered Milch's dense, rhythmic monologues with a commanding cadence.

With a Machiavellian presence, McShane could have Swearengen command a room atop a balcony, offering a cold calculation like a mob boss. To play Swearengen, an actor with the same power and mastery of the text is required. An actor who loves a good antihero and could reinvent brutal, eloquent, fierce loyalty is Rufus Sewell. He has built a career playing characters who operate in moral grey areas. From his chillingly calculated performance as John Smith in The Man in the High Castle to the charismatic yet deeply flawed Hal Wyler in The Diplomat, he brings a dark magnetism to the screen. Sewell has an imposing, intense gaze and powerful vocal delivery that can pivot from quiet threat to an explosive outburst. As an accomplished stage actor, Sewell has the linguistic dexterity to tackle Swearengen's text. Sewell could easily pull back the layers of the monstrous man to reveal the broken humanity underneath.

One of the more tragic and human characters in the series was Alma Garret. She arrived in the South Dakota gold-mining camp with her husband, Brom Garret (Timothy Omundson), to seek their fortune. After her husband's suspicious death, which was orchestrated indirectly through camp politics, she inherited a rich gold claim, establishing her own bank and independence, as she was not going to be pushed out by men. She struggled with and overcame an addiction to laudanum, had a complicated romance with Seth Bullock, but ultimately formed a unique family when she married prospector Whitney Ellsworth (Jim Beaver) and adopted a young orphan girl named Sofia Metz (Bree Seanna Wall). Bringing a quiet, nuanced strength to a complex frontier world, Molly Parker was anything but fragile. She shone by showing Alma's resilience, then shifting into an emotional palette comprised of fear and grief.

At her core, Alma was a brilliant character because she celebrated her determination, savviness, and sharpness. She had to mature into an independent woman quickly, yet still had an inherent youthful refinement. An actress who could capture that same emotional intelligence is Hailee Steinfeld. And wouldn't it be perfect since her rise to stardom came in a heavy Western, True Grit? Even at a young age, Steinfeld proved she could handle the demanding, rhythmically complex dialogue of high-end Westerns. Alma needs a duality of high-society and internal gruffness. Steinfeld has shown those traits in projects like Dickinson and The Edge of Seventeen, playing women constrained by societal environments but with a rebellious edge. Having previously played in male-heavy, ensemble-driven environments like the Marvel Cinematic Universe and Bumblebee, Steinfeld could capture Alma's core arc of navigating a camp run by aggressive, dangerous men.

Four icons and four completely different ways of walking into trouble. This is the one you would have been out there, why it fits, and what it would have cost you.

You have never explained yourself and you have never needed to, because people work out fairly quickly that you are the most capable person in the room without you ever having to mention it.

You are the funniest person in most rooms and the most loyal person in all of them, and you would be genuinely insulted if somebody suggested those two things were in any tension.

You never wanted this job, and you took it anyway because the alternative was standing there watching somebody less careful do it badly, which is the most Western reason there has ever been.

Nobody has ever successfully talked you out of something you had already decided was right, and a long line of grown adults has found that out the hard way.

There were certainly plenty of bad guys in Deadwood, but there was one man known for being beloved and ethical: Whitney Ellsworth. The outspoken prospector began his journey as a loquacious man; then, when he witnessed Dan Dority (W. Earl Brown) murder Brom Garret, he swooped in to help Alma Garret with her gold claim, ultimately finding a marriage arrangement that changed their relationship and formed a surrogate family in a camp of individuality. His fate was sadly sealed when he went toe-to-toe with George Hearst (Gerald McRaney), standing up for what was right. But in the Wild West, adversity can lead to death. Ellsworth's death remains one of the most devastating events in the entire story. Jim Beaver transformed the minor, unkempt prospector into an emotionally resonant hero. Beaver found a tenderness in Ellsworth that matched the working-class dignity that gave the camp warmth.

Ellsworth was created with great charisma and unpretentious decency. Whoever plays the part must be able to transform from heavy-drinking gold miner to beloved father figure. Shea Whigham would be a perfect fit, as he possesses the exact blend of working-class grit, underlying nobility, and tragic weariness required to build Ellsworth. Whigham has a history in Westerns, having previously appeared in American Primeval and Lawman: Bass Reeves. He wears the dirt, sweat, and moral ambiguity of the Old West naturally. Ellsworth is a tragicomic character, and Whigham has created a career playing deeply flawed men wrestling with guilt and searching for redemption. His performances in Boardwalk Empire and Lake George prove he can tackle an individual beaten down by life but still capable of good. Whigham's masterful resume in ensemble pieces is further proof that his generosity would be welcome in this profoundly brilliant series.

The camp was filled with a lot of eccentric personalities, often defined by their trade. As one of the most needed individuals in Deadwood, Dr. Amos "Doc" Cochran was the sole physician in the mining camp. A former Civil War battlefield doctor, Doc is deeply scarred by violence, suffering, and amputations witnessed during the war. He is honest, compassionate, and moral, treating anyone who needs help, regardless of their status, creed, or background. Doc had some wonderful moments during his time, including inventing a specialized leg brace attached to a boot for the character Jewel (Geri Jewell), but that was countered by a bout with tuberculosis and an affinity for grave robbing. Played by Brad Dourif, he balanced deep, raw empathy with a weary, nervous intensity that allowed Doc to feel richly human. Dourif brought a layered performance to the series, finding nuances with each character he shared a scene with. Whether being berated by Swearengen or having a delightfully playful bond with Jewel, Doc's presence was always spelled fun.

To take on Doc in a new iteration, you need an actor with the same decency. He must have instinctual intelligence, yet emotional honesty. A great option would be Ben Whishaw. He has the precise blend of raw vulnerability, sharp intellect, and world-weary cynicism to step into his iconic boots. As the sole medical authority in camp, Doc needs to be dependable, yet never let that go to his head. Whishaw showed that same energy in This Is Going to Hurt. His portrayal of a brilliant, utterly depleted, and stressed doctor managing a broken system perfectly mirrors Doc's overwhelmed, yet deeply dedicated bedside manner. Whishaw has played men with fractured psyches, including The Hollow Crown, which would allow him to tap into the trauma that Doc carries. While Dourif brought a wild-eyed, older eccentricity to the character, Whishaw would have a bit more youthful energy. That said, as Q in the James Bond franchise, his younger brilliance allowed him to go toe-to-toe with tyrants he's not intimidated by.

There was always someone offering kindness in Deadwood, and often, it was Sol Star. Arriving alongside Seth Bullock, he was a natural entrepreneur, and unlike many opportunistic figures, Sol served as a steady, practical, and compassionate moral conscience. Based on the real-life Jewish immigrant, John Hawkes brought a quiet, genuine, gentlemanly quality to the story. He navigated the subtle camp prejudices authentically. He may not have been the biggest personality on the series, but when he was on the screen, he brought brilliant intrigue. Hawkes played the perfect narrative foil throughout the show's run.

While he wasn't a visibly active player later in its run, Sol's grounded nature was crucial when times got tough. For a new iteration, we need an actor with a blend of quiet intensity, period-authentic gravitas, and empathetic nuance. Look no further than Ben Rosenfield. With an extensive pedigree in HBO period drama, his run on Boardwalk Empire as Willie Thompson proves he can deliver stylized, complex dialogue without losing the natural human emotion behind it. Where Bullock can be hot-headed, Sol is calculating and deeply pragmatic. Rosenfied had to navigate a similar dynamic as Willie. He's also shown a tender side while playing John Schlafly in Mrs. America. While excelling at characters who use intellect, restraint, and tactical patience, he would bring the right energy for a man who needs to be smart to survive a lawless land. As an actor from a Jewish family, Rosenfeld may bring a personal connection to Sol's identity and resilience.

What Deadwood did particularly well was never skimping on reality. If that meant violence, they showed it. If it meant showcasing "sinful" lives to survive, it was presented. In Deadwood at the time, women were relegated to some very unfortunate positions to survive, which included sex work. Perhaps the most prominent figure in that world was Trixie. She was a sharp-tongued prostitute at Swearengen's Gem Saloon. As his favorite girl, she shared a complex, volatile, and deeply codependent dynamic with him, marked by both abuse and unspoken affection. Over the course of the series, Trixie evolved, found her independence, and found love with Sol Star. Paula Malcomson made her a standout character with an impeccable performance. She smartly avoided the "hooker with a heart of gold" cliché by making her sharp, intelligent, and fiercely resilient. Her fight for her agency was what made her a profound character, and one to truly champion.

Growing from a traumatized young woman into a fervently protective moral compass gave her one of the most natural arcs of any character. Yet, no matter where she was in life, Trixie never lost her edge. To bring a similar ferocity and the same exact emotional depth, Dominique Fishback would be perfection. Fishback's greatest strength as an actress is mastering vulnerability wrapped in armor. Trixie's defining trait is her extreme defense mechanisms. When she launches into contextless, explosive tirades, it's meant to mask deep trauma. In The Deuce, Fishback played a part that mirrored much of this attitude. As Darlene, a 1970s sex worker, she navigated an incredibly harsh, predatory environment, yet refused to let others crush her. Able to take on darker parts, her Emmy-nominated performance in Swarm highlighted how she can lean into uncomfortable, deeply intense, psychologically rich moments. Fishback is one of the most transformative actresses of the generation. She can play tender, like in Judas and the Black Messiah, and gritty, like in Project Power, but her ability to tackle Milch's text was proven in her one-woman show Subverted. She can capture the rhythmic cadence and weight of the melodic Western text that would make her iteration of Trixie more powerful than ever before.

Few people were as strong an advocate for the women in the camp as Joanie Stubbs. She began her tenure as the savvy, high-level madam of the Bella Union gambling hall and saloon, working alongside and confiding in the manipulative Cy Tolliver (Powers Boothe). She eventually broke away from his control by opening up her own upscale brothel and schoolhouse, Chez Ami. Kindhearted and empathetic, Joanie carries deep emotional trauma from a severely abusive childhood when she was sold into servitude. During her time, she established a tender romantic and emotional bond with Calamity Jane (Robin Weigert). Brought to life by Kim Dickens, she ensured the hardened woman had a soul inside. She mastered the dense text, making it feel colloquial. Dickens' greatest strength was finding subtle humor, giving Joanie a bit of dry wit amidst the weight of the world.

The most important quality anyone playing Joanie needs is the ability to align with her complex, melancholic, and deeply resilient nature. Having quite a resume playing quiet, grounded, emotionally rich women, Kerry Condon would excel in the part. Whether playing the grieving Stacey Ehrmantraut in Better Call Saul or capturing the ticking-clock internal panic of Kay Summersby in Pressure, Condon excels at conveying a character's internal, unspoken pain. Joanie has a duality: an empathetic woman and a business powerhouse. That was on full display as Siobhan Suilleabhain in The Banshees of Inisherin, capturing the sharp-tongued, no-nonsense exhaustion while remaining morally centered. Having extensive experience of performing Shakespeare, she would ease into tackling the Western Shakespeare of Milch's text. Period pieces are also Condon's forte. From Octavia in the lavishly decadent historical drama Rome to the melancholic wilderness drama Train Dreams, Condon's ability to slip into Deadwood's period setting would make her an absolute MVP.

Part of the dynamics that made Deadwood a deliciously delectable story was watching the variety of individuals who called the camp home. The show began with a few new factions of characters arriving, shaking up Deadwood's natural order. One prominent trio was Charlie Utter, Calamity Jane, and the infamous Wild Bill Hickok (Keith Carradine). Honoring the history of the West, the show killed off Wild Bill quite early on, which came as a shock. In turn, it allowed Charlie to grow into a brilliant individual. He is the loyal, longtime friend and traveling companion who tries to manage his heavy drinking and gambling while staying solvent. He is deeply honest, morally uptight, and famously blunt, tough, and fearless in the face of danger. If there are scoundrels in camp, Charlie will sniff them out. Dayton Callie brought an impressive, gritty, world-weary loyalty that grounded the moral core. Callie kept Charlie as the ethical compass. Callie infused the character with a weathered physicality, bringing a slightly hunched posture, observant eyes, and the quiet dignity of a man who has survived hard miles.

When examining what Deadwood might look like should it be cast in 2026, one can't help but ponder who from the original cast could return in new parts. As much as it would be juicy to see Olyphant back, he would likely play a villain, and fans would scowl at that prospect. So the next best option would be former Sol Star, John Hawkes. There are a few parts that he could tackle in his later career, but the most interesting would be his take on Charlie Utter. Hawkes now has the perfect blend of grit and gravitas for seasoned character parts. He fits the practical, dirt-under-the-fingernails frontiersman perfectly. Charlie needs to shift into comedic, pathetic, and dangerous aspects without ever losing his humanity, and he has to be capable of being pushed to violence. Hawkes can do that. From menacing, rugged Teardrop in Winter's Bone to the lonely, broken Hank Prior in True Detective: Night Country, Hawkes has range.

At first, it seemed as if Al Swearengen was meant to be the typical villain of the story. But then, when Cy Tolliver arrived, Swearengen became a richly complex protagonist because Tolliver was the real antagonist in Deadwood. Well, before George Hearst said, "Hold my beer." Tolliver is a former riverboat gambler, con man, and pimp who arrives to open the Bella Union, a direct rival of the Gem Saloon. He is a cold-blooded individual with a passionate desire to carve out his own share of the town's lucrative gold-rush wealth and vice trade. Tolliver is a deeply calculating individual whose inner motivations remain famously opaque. Powers Boothe's display of villainy was so menacing due to his imposing physical presence, chilling, cold-blooded intelligence, and trademark full-body sneer. He did lean into that mustache-twirling trope, but that's why he was so incredible when he went toe-to-toe with McShane's Swearengen.

Boothe had a mix of dark humor, calculating ambition, and an underlying fragility driven by obsession and power plays. He was one of the most Shakespearean figures in camp. As fascinating as Olyphant would be in the part, we'll give it to the master of bad guys, Danny Huston. He is known for bringing a sinister swagger and magnetic malice that has made him one of the best actors to play antagonists. He has a silver-tongued charm, casual cruelty, and aristocratic essence that fit the venomous Tolliver. Having made a career out of playing deeply corrupt men of power, as seen in Succession and Wonder Woman, Tolliver would be old hat. He's also tackled Westerns in the past, as seen in Horizon: An American Saga and Yellowstone. Huston would bring a weathered, elder-statesman quality that would help him capture the essence of a man who threatens to fight the encroaching civilized world.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Scaroni explains Cardinale’s ‘long-term’ project and why stadium is ‘fundamental’ for success

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Paolo Scaroni has insisted that AC Milan have a long-term project under Gerry Cardinale, for which the stadium is a vital component.

As we wrote about at length last night, Cardinale has big plans to help get Milan back towards the top end of European football. The new ‘MSE’ holding company is the latest development, while the Generali Stadium was recently unveiled, and other projects like the NBA Europe team are ongoing.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

It is all part of a mission to try and make the Rossoneri brand more than just a football club. Scaroni has been the face of the stadium project for many years, and he was a guest at a sports talk organised by RCS and entitled ‘Attracting foreign capital and enhancing the system for a real relaunch’.

He spoke alongside Giovanni Carnevali (CEO and General Manager of Juventus), Giuseppe Marotta (President and CEO of Inter) and Ezio Maria Simonelli, (President of the Lega Calcio Serie A). His words are via MilanNews.

Serie A seems to be struggling compared to other Italian sports. What could be done to make football more competitive?

“You’re telling me about something we’re doing, Gerry Cardinale’s idea. He came to the world of Italian football after twenty-five years in sport.

“It’s about creating Milan Sport Entertainment, that is, creating other sports around Milan, combining other sports with football, providing entertainment, communicating, and using Milan and Milano – because he also places great importance on the city of Milan, which is one of Europe’s emerging cities – to create an attractive environment for other sports and other situations.

“I’ll give just one example: if you take the United States, which is a gigantic market for the world of sports, football comes in fifth among the sports that Americans love, after basketball, of course, and even after hockey.

“So, how do we make football – and, in our case, Serie A – attractive in the United States? Precisely by creating this concept of entertainment around football, this concept of Milan, of fashion, of spectacle, that is, creating an attractiveness that competes with the other sports we compete with every day.

“This is the idea that Gerry brought in Italy, coming from his vast experience in the world of sport, and therefore around this we look to our future. Gerry Cardinale comes to say that he will never sell Milan, precisely because his project is a long-term one.”

The Generali Stadium will be something more than a stadium and will help this…

“Let’s say it’s been quite a complex journey. A very long one that I’ve been on with Inter since 2018-2019. But we’ve reached, let’s say, some key milestones. The first was in November last year, when we bought the San Siro stadium.

“The Meazza belongs to Inter and Milan, and the entire surrounding area. So every now and then I read debates: ‘But should it be torn down, shouldn’t it be torn down?’. Well, this will be a decision we’ll make because it’s ours, so I want to say: we have a clear, notarial fact behind us that gives us ownership of the San Siro stadium.

“This was the first step. The second was the presentation of the stadium that we held a few weeks ago. I think the Milanese and the professionals liked this presentation of this stadium designed by two great architects: one who worked on the exterior of the stadium and another who was responsible for the division of the spaces inside.

“Building a great stadium like this is something extremely important. Technical. Because then, as always when you tackle new topics, you learn things. I, for example, learned that Manica – this company that designs our stadium – has built six hundred stadiums around the world.

“So, to say that building a stadium is a profession, it’s not something you improvise. And this was the second step. The third was that Generali seized the opportunity to own this new stadium and the old one as well, because the collaboration on the Generali Stadium begins now.

“They seized the opportunity to have the two great Milanese clubs in Milan, both of which have a history of victories, are known worldwide and have millions of fans worldwide, and to use this new stadium to promote their image, I would say especially in Europe – because Generali are a very European company – but I would also say worldwide.

“They seized a great opportunity because opportunities like this are just the ones you get. And so I must say the negotiations concluded very successfully.”

“We have all the authorisations in front of us by September 2027. Therefore, in principle, we could begin construction of the new stadium between the end of 2027 and the beginning of 2028. So, let’s say, we’re pretty close to the home stretch.

“Naturally, as always with these issues, there may be some discussion points. Furthermore, in Milan we’re heading towards an election campaign for a new mayor, and a topic like the construction of a new stadium is one that can naturally excite politicians.

“Let’s say we’re confident because the entire process we’ve taken so far protects us from these potential disruptions, and we’re fiercely determined to move forward. With the new stadium, among other things, we’ll have €100 million in additional revenue.

“This €100 million in additional revenue is a fundamental ingredient for the success of AC Milan, Inter Milan, and Serie A around the world, because ultimately, €100 million – to have great players, to pay great players – is a fundamental ingredient for success.”
