Fingers Point to RFK Jr. as U.S. Loses Measles Elimination Status

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

On Tuesday, the Pan American Health Organization announced that the United States, as well as Bolivia and Mexico, had lost their measles elimination status, a situation that requires “urgent attention,” said Dr. Jarbas Barbosa, director of PAHO, at a press conference. “Elimination must be continuously protected.”

Calling measles “probably the most contagious virus,” he said that vaccination is “the main measure to protect” against it, and that vaccination coverage must remain “very high, above 95 percent” in every community.

Amid sinking vaccination rates, the same strain of the virus has now circulated unabated in the U.S. for a year, meaning that the virus is now endemic. This determination ends America’s hard-earned measles elimination status, achieved in 2000, which had been unequivocal proof that the country was doing something right in its effort to thwart spread of the disease.

The statistics are stunning. As of November 12, according to records maintained by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, there were 5,412 cases of measles reported in 2026, spread across 46 states and the District of Columbia, and 10 deaths.

Contrast that with 2024, the final year of the Biden administration, which saw a total of 285 cases.

The U.S. is not alone in seeing a rise in cases.  As The Atlantic pointed out, “In January, six other countries — including Austria, Spain, and the United Kingdom — lost their elimination status. Measles was declared endemic in Canada again last year.”

But, despite a worldwide decline in vaccinations attributed to the lost momentum of the Covid-19 pandemic and rising public-health mistrust, it’s not impossible to earn or maintain the measles elimination status conferred by regional commissions of the World Health Organization.

Cabo Verde, Mauritius, and the Seychelles did it last year, eliminating measles and rubella and becoming the “first sub-Saharan countries to attain this milestone,” according to a November 2025 WHO press release. The method was not rocket science, but increased vaccination and improved disease surveillance. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

However, when the wealthiest country on Earth, once a public-health powerhouse that deployed disease detectives and other experts around the world to help control outbreaks, loses its measles elimination status, it is likely proof that the country is doing something wrong.

And that wrong thing is having Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as health secretary, Sen. Angela Alsobrooks (D-Md.) tells Rolling Stone, calling his tenure “an utter disgrace” that has “rolled back 30 years of progress.” She adds, “Our health is now held hostage by the lies of a conspiracy theorist. Countless American lives will continue to be lost because of RFK Jr.’s disastrous tenure.”

In a statement, CDC Director Dr. Erica Schwartz downplayed the significance of the change in status: “The measles elimination designation is not what protects Americans. Vaccination does. We know where transmission is occurring and we’ve invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach those communities. Losing a designation doesn’t mean we abandon an evidence-based approach. It means we remain focused on stopping outbreaks.”

A Health and Human Services spokesperson shared a quote from Dr. Barbosa of PAHO, commending the United States for strengthening its surveillance and vaccination efforts.

The news first broke last Friday that the U.S. had lost its measles elimination status, the same day that PAHO notified Kennedy of its determination in a letter. It cited a single measles chain of transmission that began on January 20, 2025 – the day that President Trump was sworn in – and persisted for more than 12 months.  Related Content Hayden Panettiere's Mother Blames Brian Hickerson For Star’s Death Kid Rock's Nashville Bar Sued After Bouncers Allegedly Beat Patron Who 'Tapped' Guitar Trump Brushes Off FAA, Microsoft, Bank Outages: ‘It’s Life. It’s Fine’ Lawsuit Accusing Selena Gomez and Wondermind Co-Founders of Fraud Is Dismissed

While the letter noted “significant efforts undertaken by the United States to manage the measles outbreak” through better surveillance and vaccination, it encouraged the U.S. to  “intensify these efforts until transmission is interrupted and the outbreak is fully controlled.”

Even Republican Senator Bill Cassidy (R-La.) repudiated Kennedy, telling Face the Nation host Margaret Brennan on Sunday, “This has been a failure. If I were president, I would choose someone else because there needs to be accountability for higher health care costs, more measles deaths, losing elimination status, and no progress on processed foods.”

As chairman of the U.S. Senate Committee on Health, Education, Labor, and Pensions, it was Cassidy who provided the final vote to advance Kennedy’s nomination as health secretary, after securing a series of fig-leaf promises from him that he would not alter the CDC’s existing vaccine recommendations.

But from a bully pulpit, a lot of undermining is possible. Even as Kennedy first took office, he downplayed the measles outbreak in West Texas and then recommended vitamin A, instead of a tried-and-true method: vaccination and disease surveillance. That decision led at least one newly appointed HHS official to quit.

All the chin-ups in airports, a laissez-faire swim in a contaminated creek, the podcasts, cooking shows, and cameo appearances with now-adjudicated rapist Russell Brand, cannot substitute for the stark fact that one of the world’s most wildly infectious diseases, ruled eliminated 25 years ago, is back under Kennedy’s watch.

Though the post-Covid decline in vaccinations and the accompanying rise in measles cases began under the Biden administration, the failure to effectively address this explosion in cases undeniably belongs to Kennedy, says Dr. William John Moss, a professor in the departments of epidemiology at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health.

The blame for the longstanding pockets of low vaccine coverage around the country, and the number of infections in unvaccinated adults cannot be laid at the feet of the current administration, Moss says. But Kennedy is unequivocally to blame for the federal response to the measles outbreak. “We’ve just seen mixed messaging, a focus on treatment rather than vaccination, and an executive order from the president that undermines confidence in the MMR vaccine.” He adds, “The response had to be much stronger. RFK Jr. has worked a long time to undermine public trust in vaccines.”

Even in the run-up to the 2024 election, Donald Trump and Kennedy bonded over vaccine skepticism, as a prelude to their joining forces. In a leaked video of the two men talking, Trump could be heard wooing Kennedy by saying of an allegedly excessive childhood-vaccination shot, “It looks like it’s meant for a horse, not a 10-pound or 20-pound baby.”

However, being chastened by failure is not really a thing in this iteration of the Trump administration. As Kennedy told a recent gathering of his Make America Healthy Again supporters, “The Senate has no leverage over me anymore.” It is not clear what further unfortunate milestones could yet be achieved.

Despite ample evidence that the MAHA coalition is crumbling amid the devastation of midterm Republican losses and a series of unkept promises on banishing ultra-processed food and pesticides, Stephen Miller’s ascent as Trump’s chief of staff — a perceived backer of the MAHA agenda — clearly put a spring in Kennedy’s step.  Trending Stories Watch Buffalo Springfield Come Together for the First Time in Many Years Jennifer Lopez Teases New Music on ‘Fallon’: ‘This Does Feel Like a Happy Era’ Cynthia Erivo, Travis Barker Honor Phil Collins With Big Vocals, Big Drums at Rock Hall Ceremony Priscilla Presley Scores Partial Win Over Fraud Suit, But Claims About Lisa Marie’s Death Can Stand

That defiance will run headlong into a Democratic majority in Congress, come the new year, and losing the measles elimination status will likely top the bill of particulars in any effort to impeach the health secretary.

Incoming U.S. Senator Dr. Annie Andrews (D-S.C.), a pediatrician, has pledged repeatedly to do just that, calling him a “clear and present danger to public health here in America.”

Kalshi paused Trump mention markets at CFTC's request over manipulation risk

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The CFTC has stayed certification of some Kalshi mention contracts, citing possible manipulation, two months after a staff advisory

Kalshi halted trading on several contracts centered on President Donald Trump's remarks late last week after the Commodity Futures Trading Commission asked it to, according to CNBC.

The paused contracts invite traders to bet on Trump's use of nicknames ahead of 2027, his word choices in November, and the countries and companies he mentions before this month closes, according to CNBC.

Brooke Nethercott, a CFTC spokesperson, told CNBC the agency has put a hold on certifying several of the mention markets Kalshi has proposed. She pointed to a contract on which key words Trump would utter ahead of a set deadline, one that Kalshi self-certified in February 2025.

"The CFTC stayed the certification of these contracts because they may be susceptible to manipulation, which is consistent with the staff advisory the CFTC put out two months ago," Nethercott said, according to CNBC.

Staff of the CFTC's Division of Market Oversight issued the advisory on Sept. 22. It covers contracts that settle on whether an individual will say certain words, attend an event or interact with another person. The staff said they may view such contracts as presumptively readily susceptible to manipulation and expect exchanges to make a heightened showing to list them. The advisory states it represents only the views of staff and does not create new obligations.

The advisory says those closest to the outcome often have advance knowledge, such as access to scripts or prepared remarks, and can be targeted by others seeking to influence the result.

Kalshi and the White House did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

The CFTC was looking into mention markets internally in August, according to CNBC. The agency asked Kalshi to pull its mention markets tied to sports; CNBC describes the company as the sole U.S.-regulated exchange that lists this kind of contract. Kalshi complied.

A mention market tied to Trump came under scrutiny in July, when a White House teleprompter operator was found to have traded on the president's comments, according to CNBC. In a settlement, the CFTC ordered the operator to stop trading for three years and pay a penalty of more than $172,000.

A mention market on what Vice President JD Vance will say at the New York Times DealBook Summit on Wednesday was active as of Tuesday morning, according to CNBC.

Cologne Auction House Moves Forward with Sale of Gothic Paintings as Jewish Dealer’s Heirs Claim They Were ‘Stolen’

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Cologne-based Lempertz auction house said it still planned to sell two works by Gothic painter Johann Koerbecke after the heirs of a German Jewish art dealer who was persecuted by the Nazis claimed the pieces were “stolen.”

Dealer Max Stern held the paintings in Düsseldorf between 1936 and 1937, the year the Nazis shut down his gallery. Per a provenance supplied by Lempertz, both paintings were then held by Düsseldorf’s Galerie Hans Bammann, which appears to have then sold them. Related Articles Celebrating the Inaugural Art Week NYC, Organized by ARTnews, Art in America, and Artforum New Museum Inks Multiyear Partnership with Video Art-Focused Vega Foundation

On Monday, the Dr. Max and Iris Stern Foundation issued a release in which it called on Lempertz to stop the sale of the two works planned for Saturday. According to the foundation, such a sale should not occur “until two distinct ownership claims of different victims are justly resolved according to the Washington Principles on Nazi-Confiscated Art.”

Both paintings—The Annunciation and The Vision of Saint Bernard—are likely from Marienfeld high altarpiece, which was completed in 1457. They are being auctioned in a sale of Old Masters art. They carry high estimates of around 300,000 euros each, or about $329,000 apiece, making them the auction’s most expensive works. “For art historical reasons,” the Lempertz lot entries read, “the two important panels (Lots 1005 and 1006) will first be offered individually and then together.”

The Dr. Max and Iris Stern Foundation pointed out that both works appear in several databases of missing artworks, including the Interpol Database of Stolen Art since 2021. On the German Lost Art Database, both are reported as “Nazi-confiscated property”; the foundation registered that claim with the organization in 2018.

The foundation has demanded the return of multiple artworks belonging to Stern over the past decade, and in some cases, it has been successful. In 2017, for example, the FBI returned a painting by Jan Franse Verzijl that had been on view at an US art fair two years earlier.

Potentially complicating the matter is the fact that, prior to Stern’s possession of the Koerbecke paintings, they were owned by Ellen Funke, who, during the Nazi era, was classified as a Mischling, or “mongrel,” because she had Jewish ancestry through her mother. She sold her collection in 1936 to fund her emigration from Germany, and works she once owned have also been the subject of restitution claims—two of which happen to involve the Koerbecke works.

In 2025, Bavaria’s state museums restituted Koerbecke’s Vision of Saint Bernard to Funke’s heirs. At the time, the Bayerische Staatsgemäldesammlungen noted that The Vision of Saint Bernard and The Annunciation were at one point part of a single work, with both images appearing on either side of the same panel, and that Stern had taken them apart with the hope of selling them both. Funke’s heirs are listed as the sellers of The Vision of Saint Bernard on the auction house’s website.

Similarly, Funke’s heirs are also listed as the sellers of the The Annunciation, which entered the Kolumba Art Museum of the Archdiocese of Cologne as a donation in 1996. The museum restituted the work in 2025.

The Dr. Max and Iris Stern Foundation’s release notably does not mention Funke by name. But it does state that the foundation “maintains that the rights and histories of both Jewish victims must be taken into account, and neither claim should be privileged at the expense of the other.” In an email to ARTnews, a spokesperson for the foundation said “the museums restituted the paintings to Ellen Funke’s heirs in accordance with the priority principle set forth in the guidelines established in Germany to apply the Washington Principles,” adding that that restitution ”did not result in a ‘just and fair solution’ in accordance with the Washington Principles.”

In a statement to ARTnews, Lempertz said it would not cancel its planned sale of the paintings by Koerbecke, who is widely considered one of the great painters of the Gothic era in Germany.

“We adhere to current restitution practices and follow the recommendations of the Federal Government,” a representative for the auction house said, claiming that the Stern “had the panels sawn in two” and went on to sell one of them for “double the price.”

“These magnificent paintings were not taken away from Max Stern,” the Lempertz representative said, adding, “Max Stern received compensation after the war; the heirs of the Funke family did not.”

The Stern Foundation spokesperson said the “view that the paintings were not ‘seized’ from Max Stern has long been refuted,” citing a 2019 statement from the now-defunct German Advisory Commission that the persecution of Jewish art dealers began at latest by August 1933, “so that from that point on at the latest, it can in no way be assumed that ‘proper, customary business transactions’ took place.” The spokesperson added that the claim that Max Stern was compensated for the paintings is “incorrect“ and has “no legal basis.”

Lempertz did not dispute the listings for both paintings on the Art Loss Register, but the representative also noted that the listings contain details of the works’ respective restitutions.

Bitwise CIO Sees Bitcoin Capital Rotating Into Zcash as Institutions Seek Privacy

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The celebrated analyst expects ZEC to see strong record runs in the future, driven by what he views as a developing Bitcoin-to-Zcash rotation narrative.

For the better part of this year, Zcash (ZEC) gained notable traction, which enabled it to rise from a low of $188 (witnessed on Feb.6) to a massive multi-year high of $1,684 (recorded on Sept.26). Linking further possibilities, capital market analyst Matt Hougan highlighted a connection between Bitcoin and the privacy-focused cryptocurrency. In a podcast aired Sunday, the Bitwise CIO (chief investment officer) outlined a possible case for rotating Bitcoin capital into Zcash.

In the interview, Hougan revealed that privacy is the latest important component in on-chain finance. According to him, this element is becoming increasingly crucial, particularly in the crypto landscape where data can be visible on-chain.

As traditional assets move on-chain, institutional investors may miss the element of confidentiality in the DeFi world, he narrated. He said this pursuit may prompt institutions to adopt privacy-focused crypto solutions like Zcash, Monero, and others.

Talk of a potential Bitcoin-to-Zcash rotation picked up momentum this week from the Bitwise executive, sparking further excitement in the already optimistic crypto narrative. The basis of this call is that as institutions continue moving RWAs onchain, they will require privacy-preserving DeFi protocols to protect user data. That’s where Zcash comes into the spotlight.

Hougan anticipates that as OG crypto holders diversify from older crypto assets into privacy solutions, institutional investors, especially Bitcoin whales, will join the wave. He, however, foresees a selective rotation rather than a complete institutional surrender.

Hougan’s optimistic views signal significant market shifts. His comments show that as institutional capital enters the on-chain space, the complete transparency of public blockchains may pose a major obstacle for serious players.

The well-known analyst highlighted the importance of confidential mechanisms, specifically Zcash.

The Bitcoin-to-Zcash rotation narrative appears to be on the horizon. Recently, several early Bitcoin whales urged early Bitcoin investor Dan Held to buy Zcash months before ZEC topped $1,000 in September. Also, earlier last month, recognized Bitcoin whales, the Winklevoss brothers, pointed out their radical shift to Zcash.

Broadway’s Seaview Taps New York-Based Exec & Former Clinton Non-Prof Advisor Justin Klein As COO; Ups Anna Mack Pardee

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Seaview, the Tony-, Olivier- and Peabody Award winning production company has tapped Justin Klein, a New York-based executive and former advisor to Hillary Rodham Clinton and her nonprofit organization Onward Together, as its new Chief Operating Officer, with senior Seaview producer Anna Mack Pardee named to the newly created role of Head of Theater.

The changes come as the company focuses on developing the infrastructure for its next phase of multimedia development. As Head of Theater, Pardee will lead the company’s largest department, with a company line-up this Broadway season to include Lost Lear starring Jessica Chastain, the current It’s a Wonderful Life: A Live Radio Play, and the Off Broadway revival of Spring Awakening at their Studio Seaview.

In a statement, Seaview’s co-founder and CEO Greg Nobile said, “We are so excited to welcome Justin to Seaview as our Chief Operating Officer. As we grow across theater, film and television, we’ve been intentional about building a team with the infrastructure to take on bigger and bolder work. Justin has spent his career turning big ideas into well-run organizations across media and politics, and he’ll be the one making sure our operations can keep up with our ambitions.” Watch on Deadline

Nobile continued, “I’m also thrilled to promote Anna to Head of Theater. She has been a driving force behind so much of what Seaview has put onstage, and she has more than earned the chance to lead our largest department. I couldn’t be prouder of our team, or more excited for all that’s ahead — on Broadway and beyond.”

Since its founding in 2012, Seaview’s credits have included Slave Play, Parade, Tony Award-winning Best Play Stereophonic, Illinoise, Every Brilliant Thing, and Good Night and Good Luck starring George Clooney, with film work including Reality starring Sydney Sweeney and Stress Positions.

Klein comes to Seaview from Fenway Strategies, a strategic communications firm, where he held the COO role, overseeing the firm’s business operations, financial management, and growth strategy. Previously, he was Vice President of Business Affairs at Recount Media, and earlier in his career, he held senior roles on political campaigns and at the Democratic National Committee, and served as a Senior Advisor to Hillary Rodham Clinton and her nonprofit organization, Onward Together.

“I’ve long admired Seaview for the ambition of its work and its role in shaping contemporary theater,” Klein said in a statement. “Bringing my experience building and scaling organizations to Seaview at this stage in its evolution is incredibly meaningful. I’m thrilled to join Greg Nobile and the team at such an exciting moment for the company.”

Pardee is Seaview’s most senior producer and has been with the company for three and a half years, rising from Associate Producer to Producer and now to the newly formed role. Prior to her time with Seaview, she spent eight years at National Artists Management Company (NAMCO) with Barry and Fran Weissler, rising from assistant to Barry Weissler to Associate Producer.

Since joining Seaview, she has led the company’s producing efforts on such Broadway and Off Broadway productions, as Sam Gold’s Enemy of the People, starring Jeremy Strong and Romeo + Juliet, starring Kit Connor and Rachel Zegler; Illinoise, the Sufjan Stevens musical directed by Justin Peck; Good Night, and Good Luck; All In: Comedy About Love and All Out: Comedy About Ambition written by Simon Rich and directed by Alex Timbers; The Queen of Versailles, starring Kristin Chenoweth and directed by Michael Arden; and The Fear of 13, starring Adrien Brody and directed by David Cromer, among others. She works across Seaview’s producing and development slate, partnering with artists and creative teams from early development through production.

In its announcement, Seaview noted that the additions to its leadership team “reflect the reality of Seaview’s operations, as it has grown from a theater producer into a multi-vertical entertainment company,” adding that Klein’s “breadth of experience building and scaling organizations across media, nonprofit, and political fields will help guide Seaview in its continues expansion across across all mediums.”

In a statement, Pardee said, “Since joining Seaview, I’ve had the great privilege of working alongside an incredibly talented and passionate team guided by Greg’s innovative vision. I’m honored to step into this new role and excited to lead the next chapter of our Theater Department, championing ambitious work and the extraordinary artists who bring it to life.”

Klein and Pardee will report directly to Nobile.

Sotheby's makes last-minute deal to sell $180m Van Gogh painting following dispute

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Blaquier family, from Argentina, bought Blossoming Chestnut Trees (Châtaigniers en fleurs) (May 1890) in 1975

Sotheby’s today announced that a settlement has been reached over the sale of Van Gogh’s Blossoming Chestnut Trees (Châtaigniers en fleurs) (May 1890). The work has been poised to achieve a record price for the artist but became the subject of a claim by the heirs of its Jewish former owners, who called for the work to be withdrawn from the 18 November auction in New York.

The Sotheby’s statement records that the auction house is “pleased to confirm that an agreement has been reached between the Blaquier-Arrieta family and all of the legal descendants of Hedwig and Samuel Fischer regarding the sale of Vincent van Gogh’s Châtaigniers en fleurs”. The sale will now proceed as planned.

The work is estimated at around $180m ($207m with fees) and has been guaranteed by Sotheby's to sell. This is far in excess of the previous record of $117m, paid at Christie’s in 2022 for Van Gogh’s Orchard with Cypresses (April 1888).

The Blaquier-Arrieta family, from Argentina, bought Blossoming Chestnut Trees in 1975. As the Sotheby’s statement explains: “In recognition of the painting’s complex Nazi-era history and the suffering endured by the Fischer family during the Holocaust, the two families have reached a mutually satisfactory resolution and the work will be sold pursuant to their agreement.”

The provenance in the Sotheby’s catalogue entry notes, as it has done since last month, that the painting was acquired by the Berlin publisher Samuel Fischer and his wife Hedwig in 1909. Their son-in-law, Gottfried Bermann Fischer, deposited the Van Gogh with the Amsterdam gallery Paul Cassirer in 1932 or 1933—and it was then bought by Paris collectors Henriette and Marcel Kapferer in 1936.

Last Thursday (12 November), in a surprise move, the Berlin-based restitution researcher Gunnar Schnabel sent a letter to Sotheby’s, on behalf of the Fischer heirs. He questioned the 1936 sale—which he says took place three months after the family had left Berlin for Vienna—asking Sotheby’s to prove that the picture had been sold at its market value and that its sale was not forced. In the meantime, Schnabel asked for this week’s sale to be delayed.

In a statement, the Fischer heirs commented: “We are grateful for the trusting and fair negotiation and collaboration with Sotheby’s and the Blaquier-Arrieta family, which make it possible to acknowledge the suffering of the Fischer family during the Nazi era and honour Gisela Berman Fischer's [granddaughter of Samuel and Hedwig] history of seeking justice for her family.”

The Blaquier-Arrieta family commented: “We are pleased to have reached an amicable resolution with the Fischer heirs. We appreciate the constructive dialogue that made this resolution possible and look forward to an extraordinary result and to passing along this masterpiece to a new generation of collectors.”

No details have been released on the nature of the agreement and whether the Fischer family will receive financial compensation.

What impact, if any, the short-lived dispute will have on the sale will only become apparent with the bidding.

Looted by the Nazis, after the war it was returned to the Rothschild family

“Blossoming Chestnut Trees”, which has been hidden away for over 60 years, is being sold by Sotheby's for the Blaquier-Arrieta family of Argentina

Montmartre windmill painting is on view with Sotheby’s for the first time since it left the easel

Sotheby's sold “Parisian Novels” for $62m and “The Sower” for over $10m, a record for one of his drawings

FAA pauses installation of new routers after ground stop

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Federal Aviation Administration said Tuesday it would pause the installation of new routers after problems with the technology led the agency to issue a rare nationwide ground stop on Friday.

Tens of thousands of flights were affected by the ground stop, which halted departing flights for more than four hours. It was the longest halt to U.S. flight departures since 9/11.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said Sunday that an overloaded router installed in southern Arkansas as part of an upgrade to a system overseen by L3Harris Technologies appeared to overwhelm systems that feed radar and other data for air traffic control.

The Wall Street Journal first reported the news about the pause.

The FAA and Department of Transportation have said L3Harris was working to convert the communications network from copper wires to fiber optic.

Democratic members of the Senate Committee on Commerce, Science and Transportation this week asked the FAA and the Department of Transportation for clarity on what went wrong.

"Identifying the device that failed does not establish why it failed," the senators wrote in a statement. "Modernization cannot come at the expense of operational safety."

The agencies have said they are working to figure out the root cause of the issue and have reiterated that there is no indication of a cyberattack or outside threat.

The senators also asked for a briefing with the FAA, L3Harris and Juniper Networks, which is making the new devices, by Monday. They asked the companies to lay out why the operational systems lacked sufficient redundancy and answer other questions about what led to the outage.

CNBC has reached out to L3Harris and Juniper Networks for comment.

Duffy on Friday called for Congress to approve $50 billion in funding for upgrades to the aging U.S. air traffic control system, more than he has previously asked for. Democratic lawmakers pushed back on the request, citing unspent money from existing funding, which includes Congress' approval last year of $12.5 billion for airport and air traffic control improvements over five years.

Aging equipment and a shortage of air traffic controllers have for years angered airline executives, who have complained about the cost of resulting disruptions.

— CNBC's Phil LeBeau and Leslie Josephs contributed to this report.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/payer-mix-non-labor-expenses-dragged-hospital-margins-september" hreflang="en">Payer mix, non-labor expenses dragged hospital margins in September</a>

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Payer mix, non-labor expenses dragged hospital margins in September" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Hospitals’ operations took another dip in September amid a slew of ongoing headwinds including weakened payer mix and rising costs, healthcare advisory firm Kaufman Hall reported alongside warnings of a “historically large portion” of the nation’s hospitals running on negative or near-zero operating margins.

The firm’s latest monthly benchmark report, released Tuesday, showed a calendar year-to-date operating margin index of 1.4% and a single-month operating margin index of 2.4% in September, the latter of which was a decline from August’s 3.0% operating margin (all including health system allocations for shared services costs).

The performance decline wasn’t for want of patients. Daily discharges and adjusted discharges both rose 3% from August to September, and are generally above the numbers from last year. Operating room minutes per calendar day also rose 6% from August—an encouraging sign for hospitals whose surgical slowdowns had hamstrung acuity—but remain 1% below September of last year.

The increased foot traffic also grew revenues, with daily net operating revenue rising 4% from August and 6% year over year while gross operating revenue rose 4% month to month and 8% year over year. Much of the increase came on the outpatient side, where daily revenue increased 5% from August and 10% compared to September 2025. Net patient service revenue per adjusted discharge was more mixed, rising 2% from August but remaining 1% below September 2025.

However, hospitals’ margins are being challenged by persistent bad debt and charity care burdens, Kaufman Hall’s data show. Daily bad debt and charity care may have remained flat from August, but is 17% higher than September 2025 and 16% higher when comparing both years’ first nine months. As a percentage of gross operating revenue, they’re 6% higher than Sept. 2025 and 8% higher year to date.

On the spending side, daily total expense rose 3% from August and is 5% higher than September 2025, primarily reflecting the heightened volumes even as hospitals held their average length of stay flat month over month or 1% shorter year over year. The expenses were more pronounced on the non-labor side, where increases on a per adjusted discharge basis were higher than labor’s per adjusted discharge decline.

"Hospitals continue to operate in an environment marked by significant financial variability," Erik Swanson, managing director and Data and Analytics group leader at Kaufman Hall, said in a release. “Many organizations continue to face mounting pressure from uncompensated care and rising expenses, underscoring the importance of proactive operational and financial strategies that build long-term resilience."

Another amplifying market trend is the “bifurcation of hospital performance” often highlighted by the firm and others that monitor multi-organization financial trends, such as credit rating agencies.

"The margin gap between higher and lower-performing hospitals has widened to 19 percentage points, with a historically large portion of hospitals operating at negative or very thin margins,” the report reads. “Even slight revenue reductions threaten organizations, which face increasingly difficult decisions on future operations, care delivery, and expense control.”

The firm encouraged hospitals to take “deliberate, proactive steps” to strengthen their financial situations, particularly as macro reimbursement trends stiffen and the site of care delivery shifts from the hospital to outpatient settings.

Kaufman Hall’s hospital reports pull data from more than 1,300 nationwide hospitals as collected by parent company Strata Decision Technology.

Accompanying the monthly hospital benchmark was also Kaufman Hall’s quarterly numbers on more than 200,000 employed physicians and advanced practice providers, which painted a much rosier picture for health system and hospital employers.

It showed, for the first time in five years of data collection, that healthcare employers’ investment/subsidy per provider and per physician has declined year over year (by 1%), and “across every cohort, including primary care, medical specialty, surgical specialty and hospital-based specialty.”

Kaufman Hall attributed the rare turnaround to revenue growth and sustained productivity increases that together outpaced expenses.

"Medical groups made meaningful performance improvements this quarter, achieving gains in revenue, productivity and thoughtful workforce management," Matthew Bates, managing director and Physician Enterprise Service Line leader at Kaufman Hall, said in a release. "The growing utilization of [advanced practice providers] and ongoing focus on operational and team effectiveness are helping organizations manage financial pressures while increasing access to care.”

Democrats are preparing multiple probes of Trump's Venezuela oil deal

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

House and Senate committees are laying groundwork to examine the 35% U.S. stake in North American Blue Energy Partners

Congressional Democrats are lining up several investigations of the Trump administration’s agreement with Alejandro Betancourt’s North American Blue Energy Partners (NABEP), according to Bloomberg. Democrats on panels including the House Oversight Committee and the Senate Armed Services Committee have started preparing for those inquiries.

The agreement, announced in August, would hand NABEP a century-long concession covering 17 oil fields in Venezuela, while the U.S. would receive a 35% ownership share of the company, according to Bloomberg. The U.S. would be entitled to buy a fifth of what the fields pump, priced at cost.

Rep. Robert Garcia, a California Democrat expected to take the helm of the House Oversight Committee next year, said the aim is to establish the facts. “We want to go in there and find out exactly what happened,” Garcia told Bloomberg. He added that Democrats also want to pass legislation to prevent any abuse in the future.

Sen. Jack Reed, a Rhode Island Democrat and the top Democrat on the Senate Armed Services Committee, said his panel is among those planning investigations. “If we went in there with Maduro as an excuse, but the real goal is to take oil, that is to me wrong,” Reed told Bloomberg. Sen. Richard Blumenthal, a Connecticut Democrat set to chair the Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations, said he is concerned about “the potential for corruption.” A spokesperson for Sen. Martin Heinrich, a New Mexico Democrat expected to take over the Senate energy panel next year, said he plans to keep overseeing the deal, according to Bloomberg.

Following their midterm election victories, Democrats in both chambers will have the authority to summon administration officials for testimony, demand records and, if needed, issue subpoenas, according to Bloomberg. Their questions center on the contract's provisions, the compensation the U.S. extended to NABEP for its equity stake and the mechanism for supervising oil revenue. Democrats have also faulted the administration for keeping the text under wraps and questioned Betancourt's role, citing money-laundering and corruption probes that have targeted him in the U.S., Spain and Switzerland. Betancourt denies any wrongdoing and has faced no charges.

House Natural Resources Committee Democrats intend to carry on an inquiry launched earlier in the year, a staffer on the committee told Bloomberg. The Senate Foreign Relations Committee is also expected to launch an investigation.

Four ranking Senate Democrats, including Reed and Heinrich, had challenged the legality of the Pentagon's plan to take a 35% stake in NABEP in a letter to top administration officials. They demanded the full text of the deal before it was finalized. House Democrats sent a similar letter.

White House spokesperson Taylor Rogers defended the deal. "This deal is a major win for both the United States and Venezuela," Rogers said, according to Bloomberg. "If Democrats choose to pursue sham investigations over commonsense victories, they are choosing to work against the American people."

The administration has argued the deal would cut gasoline prices for Americans and give the government a route to restocking the strategic oil reserve, according to Bloomberg. A NABEP spokesperson told Bloomberg taxpayers paid nothing for the U.S. stake and that U.S. citizens must hold most of the seats on NABEP's board.

What We Learned When Sabastian Sawe Finally Broke the Two-Hour Marathon

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In the early 2010s, the idea of breaking two hours in the marathon went from pipe dream to attainable goal. For the first decade of the new millennium, the world’s top distance runners had been chipping away at it. Paul Tergat first breached the 2:05 barrier with his 2:04:55 mark at the 2003 Berlin Marathon. Haile Gebrselassie ran 2:03:59 at the same race in 2008, and by 2014, Dennis Kimmetto held the record with a 2:02:57, less than seven seconds per mile away from sub-two.

Like the four-minute mile, first toppled by Roger Bannister in Oxford, England, in 1954, the two-hour marathon is an enticing target. It’s an even number in a popular event. It’s easy to describe, and therefore easy to market. Perhaps that’s why Nike decided to pour millions of dollars into their Breaking2 project, starting in 2016, bringing Eliud Kipchoge within 30 seconds of the barrier on May 6, 2017, in an exhibition race on the F1 track in Monza, Italy.

After that much-watched effort, Kipchoge became the torchbearer for breaking two hours. His next attempt in 2019 came with a perfectly flat looped course, a roster of pacers blocking the wind, controlled nutrition on a controlled course, and a finishing time of 1:59:40. Kipchoge showed the world that sub-two was possible. He made history. But he didn’t do it in a race.

It would take a special set of circumstances to see the result in a record-eligible setting. An elite athlete would need the right training, the right weather conditions, the right competition. The previous nine times the world record had been set, these circumstances came together in a fall race (Berlin eight times, Chicago once). But at the 2026 London Marathon on April 26, the stage was set. Sabastian Sawe had returned to defend his title, lining up alongside 2025 Chicago Marathon champion Jacob Kiplimo and Yomif Kejelcha, an accomplished track runner making his marathon debut.

On a clear, calm day, the three runners followed pacers through the halfway mark in 60:29. To drop below two hours, they would need to run the second half at least a minute faster, cutting the pace by at least five seconds per mile from their average of 4:37. By mile 18, Kiplimo was falling off the back, leaving just Kejelcha stubbornly hanging on to the shoulder of a hard-charging Sawe. Sawe continued ratcheting the pace down, running the eighth 5K of the race (from approximately mile 21 to 24) in a blistering 13:42—4:25 per mile. But Kejelcha hung on, staying on Sawe’s shoulder until the final four minutes.

When he crossed the line in a time of 1:59:30, Sawe became the first runner to break two hours in a record-eligible marathon. Eleven seconds later, Kejelcha became the second. Kiplimo’s third place time of 2:00:28 was also faster than the previous world record. The two-hour barrier wasn’t broken by an exceptional individual in a one-off performance. Ultimately, a true race with fierce competition and constant pressure was what it took to bring Sawe into the history books.

This article is from the Winter 2026 issue of Outside magazine. To receive the print magazine, become an Outside+ member here.

Inside the rise and evolution of Ohio State's club football team

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It was a 90-degree September evening in Columbus, and the setting sun was relentlessly beating down on the Lincoln Tower Park recreational fields next to Ohio Stadium. A group of students began to dress in football pads and scarlet, white and gray colored helmets. The Ohio State marching band was wrapping up practice with a run-through of "Buckeye Battle Cry."

The players, fresh off back-to-back national championships, were about to practice next to "The Shoe." Unlike the Buckeyes who play in Ohio Stadium on Saturdays, though, nobody there was getting paid. The athletes weren't getting NIL money. They weren't on athletic scholarships. The coaches were volunteers.

What drives the Ohio State club football team is simply a love of football.

"I think it's what this program should have always been," coach James Grega Jr. said before practice. "I hope we haven't peaked."

Former and current players will tell you that Grega, who spends around 30 hours a week outside of his day job working with the club team, is a major reason for that success. By day, Grega is a content specialist at OSU's College of Arts and Sciences. In his free time, he has poured his energy into Ohio State club football, which has dominated its 15-team league, the National Club Football Association, with four championships in the past six seasons.

During the Division I varsity team's bye week, the club team went viral for beating Wright State 105-0. It set NCFA records for most points and widest margin of victory. The previous points record was 91, set by the Buckeyes two years earlier. On Nov. 15, the club team won 65-8 over Oakland to get to an 8-0 record and advance to an eighth straight conference championship.

Along with their success and massive blowout victories, six players since 2020 have gone from the Ohio State club team to the Division I team as walk ons.

Born and raised in Chicago, Illinois, Kellyn Gerenstein played quarterback from age 6 through high school. He considered a couple of Division II offers before deciding to pursue academics over athletics, enrolling in Ohio State's engineering program.

When he arrived at OSU in 2018, one of the constants in his life − playing organized football − vanished. He went through the motions at school but felt like he lacked structure. The pressure mounted. At one point, Gerenstein considered leaving Ohio State.

To kill time, Gerenstein would take a duffel bag filled with his high school football cleats and a couple of footballs from home and walk to Lincoln Tower Park late at night. He practiced throwing footballs into the available soccer nets.

"It was the only way for me to stay sane," Gerenstein said.

The kicker for the club football team was playing pickup soccer and saw Gerenstein whipping footballs around. He told Gerenstein the team needed a quarterback. Admittedly skeptical, Gerenstein showed up to the first practice and met Grega, who was embarking on his first year as coach.

Grega played on the club football team in 2012 and became the starter in 2013. Instead of playing the next year, he became the assistant sports editor of The Lantern and pursued journalism. After graduating, Grega missed being on the sideline. He had done some coaching at Westerville South High School while bouncing around writing for various sports publications.

OSU's club players reached out for Grega, hoping he would come coach them. Not only did he agree to this, but he developed a plan to reform the team's structure.

"It was very bare bones," Grega said. "So we've kind of built it. When we took over in 2018, unfortunately, the program was on the verge of not collapsing, but folding."

Grega spent time with the club sports office to ensure the team had field reservations in-season and at Ohio State's indoor recreation center. With a background in marketing and communications, Grega created a website for recruiting purposes and attempted to bolster the team's social media presence.

Along with structure, Grega wanted to instill a culture of accountability.

"It's kind of like any other team," Gerenstein said. "Like, guys have talent, but if one guy shows up more and works harder than you, we'll win with that guy. And for the most part, we didn't really run into that. All the culture and the demand changed."

That appealed to receiver Chris Booker, who had transferred from Dayton after spending time on the non-scholarship FCS program.

Booker redshirted his first year with the Flyers and was on the scout team, but he felt drained. Football felt like a job rather than the sport he grew up with.

At Ohio State, Booker focused on his degree. He played flag football as a hobby with his cousin, a fellow student.

"Hey, uh, you can still play," Booker's cousin said with assurance.

Though unconvinced, Booker met Grega and came away loving his vision for the program.

"It felt like high school ball, just playing with your friends, enjoying practice, enjoying the game, enjoying every part of football that I grew up loving," Booker said. "That was something I lacked that I got back with the club team."

During the season, Booker's younger brother passed away. The two had won a Missouri high school state championship together and shared a deep connection through football.

"It was very emotional when I was able to get back on the field again," Booker said. "It was kind of second nature for me to get back out on the field and to be able to have him in spirit with me out there."

Booker found some healing by throwing himself back into the sport.

"He was really good for us in 2018," Grega said. "I mean, heads and tails above everyone else."

In Grega's first season, the club team won its first four games before losing to the Apprentice School in the Mid-Atlantic Conference title game. Booker played in four games and caught 14 passes for 371 yards and seven touchdowns. He still holds the club record for yards per reception at 26.5.

After the season, Grega approached Booker with an idea. He edited a package of Booker's club football highlights and wanted to send the tape to a staff member on the Division I team.

"As much as I would love to have you here for three more years, you're too damn good," Grega said to Booker.

With Booker's blessing, Grega sent the tape to Justin Perez, the Buckeyes' walk-on coordinator at the time. Before long, Booker had earned a spot on the team, the first of Grega's protegees to do so.

As a walk-on, Booker played in 19 games over two seasons, mostly on special teams. He caught two passes for 27 yards in 2021. He played in both the College Football Playoff semifinal and championship games in the 2020-21 season. He also earned recognition from the team via a Special Teams Player of the Week nod in a 52-17 season-opening win against Nebraska in 2020.

"They are an elite, elite program," Booker said. "And to be able to be a part of that was surreal. ... I'm very appreciative of all the people I met there, and everybody that I developed a relationship with and everybody that made my experience better ... getting the opportunity to play in the games meant more than I could ever put in words, or express gratitude for."

Booker now works in finance and coaches at his former high school. Five years removed, he distinctly remembers the final moments of the 2021 Rose Bowl, his last game. As the confetti fell, Booker had a bittersweet feeling. He felt like the credits were rolling on a chapter of his life, but he was grateful club football had made that chapter possible.

Since Booker, five other Ohio State club football players have been elevated to the varsity program. The most recent was Jackson Gula, a safety who joined the team ahead of the 2026 season and joined former club football defensive back Glorien Gough, linebacker Zach Hayes and offensive lineman Ian Gecse on the roster.

Meanwhile, the club football team thrived. With Gerenstein under center and a vocal leader of the club, the team went undefeated in 2019 and floored most of its opponents, winning an NCFA national championship against Oakland.

Gerenstein won the NCFA's offensive MVP in 2019. Though the team did not play in 2020 due to COVID, it picked up where it left off in 2021 and won another national championship.

Gerenstein was thriving off the field as well. He changed his major from engineering to sport industry. Instead of going to the library to read his class textbooks, he would go and watch Dwayne Haskins highlights. He knew football was his path to a professional future.

In 2022, the Buckeyes lost the club national championship game to Gordon State, which was Gerenstein's last game. He finished his club football career with a 24-4 record and set several team records. His No. 4 was the first retired by the club team.

The same year, Gerenstein started a year-long recruiting internship with the Ohio State varsity team. He learned how to study film and be a part of scouting operations through Billy Homer, OSU's college scouting coordinator.

Before graduating, Gerenstein was invited by a staff member to participate in Ohio State's pro day and worked out with Ohio State players in front of NFL scouts, the only non-varsity player to do so. The players participating included future NFL players C.J. Stroud, Jaxon Smith-Njigba and Paris Johnson.

While there, he made a connection with the San Francisco 49ers and earned a scouting internship that began after graduation.

Gerenstein was hired at Boston College as an assistant director of recruiting. After a year there, Gerenstein was connected with Buffalo Bills assistant general manager Brian Gaine and became a scouting assistant in 2024. This past July, he was promoted to a combine scout recruiting players for the Bills in Georgia and the Carolinas.

"I think that club [made me] realize how much joy and passion I had for the game that I knew I loved in high school," Gerenstein. said, "but it wasn't until college that I realized I couldn't live without it."

Since taking over in 2018, Grega has created structure in the program. The club team has a robust website used for recruiting players. The team has a 10-person coaching staff with position-specific specialists. There is enough donated equipment, including pads, helmets and jerseys, for a 44-player roster. They'll even play against junior varsity squads from Division III teams; this year, the club played Wilmington College on Oct. 11, and the Buckeyes won 47-13.

Grega made a good first impression by recognizing Booker's talent and has kept a steady relationship with Tim Hinton, the executive director for football relations and special assistant to coach Ryan Day. Once or twice a year, Grega will check in with staff members such as Hinton and will provide information and tape of a player he deems a potential walk-on candidate.

Resources for the club team have vastly changed since Grega's first year. In the spring and the summer, the Division I team offers Grega equipment that would not be sellable at the pro shop. The Division I program has gifted around 70 sets of shoulder pads, along with chin straps, mouthpieces and more leftover equipment. This year, the team got a new set of jerseys, along with alternate game pants.

Grega asks players to provide their own helmets, which could cost around $500 or more for a new model. Each player also pays a $375 fee that goes toward travel fees. The club Buckeyes' first game of the season was at Michigan State, with the team taking passenger vans (driven by coaches) and staying overnight. For the in-state games, the team carpools.

Like Booker and Gerenstein, players join the club team because of their passion for competitive football. Defensive lineman Matt Humphreys, for example, was a valedictorian at his high school in Jackson, Ohio, and played FCS Division I football at Robert Morris on academic scholarship.

A pair of abdominal tears and COVID restrictions kept Humphreys off the field for all but two games of the spring in 2021 in a COVID-adjusted season. He decided it was time to transfer, but did not like any of the options and went to OSU as a regular student.

Humphreys found out about the club by scanning the school website and reached out via social media. Grega responded with a call, inviting Humphreys to a practice.

Humphreys has spent three years on the team and is a staple of the defensive line. In his final year of eligibility, he is chasing the club defensive stats records. Through four seasons, Humphreys has 56 solo tackles and 21.5 sacks. He hopes he can coach football in the future and plans to pursue a career in finance.

"With the club level, these are guys that are paying to do it, and there's no requirement to do it," Humpherys said. "The only thing they're getting out of it is enjoyment of the game."

Corbyn Aleshire spent last year on the club team as the backup quarterback. He spent four years on his high school team as a backup in Homestead, Florida. After exploring Division III options, Aleshire decided he wanted to go to Ohio State and pursue construction management. He is also a lifelong Buckeyes fan with grandparents from Lima and Dayton.

Upon arriving at Ohio State in 2025, Aleshire discovered the club football team online and filled out the recruiting form. Grega reached out to Aleshire. A year later Aleshire was in line for the starting job, and in his first home start, he had four touchdowns in a 52-6 win against George Mason.

"I thought [Grega] was a super cool dude," Aleshire said. " ... I love his offense and he's a really good salesman, that's for sure."

There have been opportunities for Grega's coaches, as well. Brendan McNeill, formerly the team's defensive line coach, was elevated to defensive coordinator. McNeill played at St. Charles Prep. He played and coached with semi-professional teams in Columbus, as well as coaching youth-level teams before meeting Grega and joining his staff.

When McNeill has time away from his information technology job and family responsibilities, he spends free time watching plays and communicating with prospective students as the club recruiting coordinator.

This year McNeill earned a two-year coaching fellowship with the United Football League through the National Coalition of Minority Football Coaches, spending last summer working with former Buckeyes Ted Ginn Jr., Larry Grant and Curtis Terry on the Columbus Aviators staff.

"I was nervous, but they pulled me right in, and that was a beautiful experience," said McNeill. "I can't wait to do it again."

Eight years in, Grega has amplified the club football team to suit the personal goals of his players and staff. He hopes he can coach a program full-time some day.

"I think a lot of people see the word 'club' on a resume and they kind of dismiss us," Grega said. "But again, that's why we work so hard to continue to build this thing, because we want to be taken seriously."

Day acknowledged the club's legitimacy at an Oct. 27 press conference.

"We've been able to get a couple of guys on the team, which has been great," Day said. "I can't sit here and say it's been a lifeblood of the program, but it is an opportunity for some guys in the program and be part of what we do here. You just never know, and maybe there's a way to build it stronger in the future."

Dan Aulbach is the Breaking & Trending Sports Reporter at the Columbus Dispatch. He can be reached at daulbach@dispatch.com or @aulbachdan on X.

These Ukrainians Aren’t in Combat. But They Deal With War Every Day.

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Over the past year, Mauricio Lima and Yurii Shyvala talked to Ukrainians in Lviv, Odesa, Kyiv and the Donbas region whose work exposes them to the suffering caused by the war.

Mykhailo Biliak has spent most of his life in the funeral business, and he no longer remembers how many graves he has dug. But he knows he has buried more than 1,200 soldiers in Lviv, in western Ukraine, since Russia started its full-scale war in 2022. “The worst day was six funerals,” said Mr. Biliak, 62.

Hundreds of miles to the east, in a basement near the front line, Daryna Boiko, a 32-year-old anesthesiologist, prepared to attach an IV drip to a soldier wounded by a drone. “It won’t be scary,” she assured him. “It was scary out there,” he replied. “It’s fine here.”

In Kyiv, the capital, Hanna Buraho, a 53-year-old psychotherapist, made tea after a session with a group of soldiers. More than 5,000 service members have been through her rehabilitation program, which uses horses. “When the war started, everyone rushed to help each other, and every person wanted to do something of their own, so this is my contribution,” Ms. Buraho said.

This Ukrainian doctor works near the front, trying to save wounded soldiers.

This man has buried more than 1,200 soldiers.

This therapist tries to help damaged veterans heal.

This forensic pathologist once helped to solve crimes. Now he identifies fallen soldiers.

In Odesa, in southern Ukraine, two morgue workers pushed open the heavy, rusted doors of a refrigerated rail carriage that contained the remains of dozens of soldiers killed in battle. A white bag was placed on an autopsy table, and Ruslan Fedorenko, 61, a forensic pathologist, turned to his work.

None of these people carry weapons. But like Ukrainians across the country, they deal with the war and its consequences every day.

178 Websites Infected in Major Optimizely Hack: Crypto Wallets Targeted, but Bitcoin Barely Reacted

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Optimizely attack infects 178 websites, steals crypto and card data, and exposes users to wallet-address swaps and iPhone exploits.

An Optimizely attack on Monday, November 16, planted crypto-stealing code on the websites of 178 organizations, including Ledger, HSBC, Zoom, the BBC and CNN. The malicious script swapped crypto wallet addresses, skimmed card numbers, and tried to break into iPhones for about 19 hours before it was cleaned up.

BREAKING: A cyberattack hit 178 major organizations, including Zoom, CNN, BBC, Fox News, HSBC, Ledger, Equifax, Salesforce, IKEA and Air France-KLM, by loading malware on the Optimizely Web platform, exposing visitors to scripts built to steal payment data and exploit iOS…

— The Hormuz Letter (@HormuzLetter) November 17, 2026

Optimizely is a tool companies use to test different versions of their web pages. An attacker slipped code into the scripts it serves, so every site that loaded them ran the malware.

We traced the attacker’s Bitcoin wallet. It received five payments worth about $5,500, and one victim alone sent nearly 90% of it. Bitcoin barely moved while the attack ran.

So what happened, and what is the real impact of hacks like this one?

Security researcher Scott Helme of Report URI found the code and published a full breakdown. The attacker wrote it into a settings field in customers’ Optimizely projects, so it was served from Optimizely’s own servers and looked legitimate.

Optimizely said it “secured affected user accounts and added protections against further unauthorized code delivery.” Report URI found no sign that Ledger hardware wallets themselves were breached. The danger was on the website, where a copied address could be swapped before a user pasted it.

Market Intelligence: Crypto Security Analyst Recommends Best Anonymous Crypto Wallets

Report URI listed the attacker’s wallets. We checked the Bitcoin one on mempool.space, the public Bitcoin block explorer.

The Optimizely attack’s impact was tiny, and the market ignored it. Bitcoin is near $64,300 after its weekend low of $62,006, and that slide came from wider selling, not this attack.

EXPLORE: Crypto Hack Losses Hit $8.74 Billion in 2026, More Than Triple 2025: How Did Bitcoin and Ether React?

The money stolen in the Optimizely attack is small, but the method is the year’s most damaging one. We sorted DefiLlama’s 2026 hack list by attack type. Attacks on websites and infrastructure, rather than on smart contracts, are now the costliest category:

These attacks hit users where no wallet setting can protect them. The fix is on the user’s side: check the address on the hardware wallet’s screen before signing, send a small test transfer first, and keep phones updated.

On the daily chart, Bitcoin trades at $64,326. It sits below its 50-day exponential moving average (EMA), an average that gives recent prices more weight, at $76,208, and its 200-day EMA at $75,864.

The Optimizely attack did not move Bitcoin, and the attacker walked away with about $7,000. Its real cost is trust: when a hardware-wallet maker’s own website can serve malware for hours, checking every address on the device is no longer optional.

Nike's Stock Is Down Big This Year. Here's When I'd Buy

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The stock has declined 40% since the start of the year, and it may still go lower.

Nike (NKE +0.71%) is a top apparel brand known for its high-quality athletic footwear. However, in recent years, it's been struggling badly. And that's putting it lightly. Down nearly 80% in five years, Nike's valuation has come crashing down due to poor financial results and a concerning outlook for the future.

The brand remains highly recognizable, and there is still strong value there. But the business itself is in a tough spot, with new CEO Elliott Hill coming on board a couple of years ago in an effort to turn things around, but there hasn't been much to show for it. The stock is trading at a discount, and rightfully so. But I wouldn't buy it just yet.

Nike reported its latest earnings numbers on Oct. 1, for the period ending Aug. 31. The results were extremely underwhelming. Revenue was down 4% year over year, and without the impact of foreign exchange, organically, the top line was down by 5%. The only positive was that in North America, it squeaked out 2% growth.

But this is as the apparel company is already going up against weak comparables from the previous year. A year ago, during the same period, it generated just 1% growth, and organically it was down by 1%.

There isn't an easy fix for the business, as younger consumers are gravitating toward cheaper clothing options from online retailers like Shein. Meanwhile, with inflation remaining elevated, economic conditions certainly aren't making it any more appealing to buy Nike's high-priced products.

Today, the stock is trading at just 18 times its trailing earnings. But even that might be a bit expensive given the challenges the business is facing. By comparison, the average stock on the S&P 500 trades at 23 times its trailing earnings.

At some point, Nike's stock becomes too cheap to pass up. Even though its growth prospects are challenging, the brand has significant value.

If the stock were to fall to $30, that's when I'd consider buying it. At that level, its market cap would be around $45 billion, putting it at a price-to-sales multiple of about one. Its price-to-earnings multiple would also drop to 14. At that low of a value, there would be an excellent margin of safety for the business.

The last time Nike's stock hit $30 was back in 2013. At that price point, the valuation could simply be too attractive to pass up.
