10 rules and cases that explain the Grammys' Best New Artist category

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Grammy Best New Artist eligibility rests on release limits and a judgment call about breakthrough, and these 10 rules and cases show how established acts still count as new

Chappell Roan released her debut single, "Good Hurt," in 2017. At the ceremony held in February 2025, she won the Grammy for Best New Artist. Those two facts show why the award confuses people.

The word "new" in the category name does not mean a first release. It does not mean a first year of making music either. The Recording Academy uses the category to recognize a breakthrough, and a breakthrough can arrive long after a debut.

Grammy Best New Artist eligibility rests on a two-part test. The first part is numerical. The Academy's published guidance says a candidate must have released at least five singles or tracks, or one album. The same guidance caps eligibility at no more than 30 singles or tracks, or three albums. Those limits leave a wide band between a brand-new act and an established one.

The second part depends on judgment. A candidate must achieve what the Academy calls a "breakthrough into the public consciousness" and have an impact on the musical landscape during the eligibility year. No chart position or sales figure defines that standard. The Academy's review process decides who meets it, and it also settles eligibility and category placement more broadly.

That mix of hard limits and soft judgment drives the confusion around the category. An artist with several albums can still qualify if the breakthrough falls in the right year. An artist with a long history can still be treated as new.

The category also has a fixed place in the Grammys' structure. It belongs to the General Field, the same group that includes Record of the Year, Album of the Year and Song of the Year.

The 10 slides that follow look at the rules first and then at specific cases. They cover the release floor and ceiling, the breakthrough standard and the gap between a career start and a breakthrough. They also cover winners and nominees who show how the rules play out, from the 2025 field to Milli Vanilli's revoked 1990 award. This is a selection of rules and cases, not a complete history of the award.

A Best New Artist candidate must have released at least five singles or tracks, or one album, under the Recording Academy's published guidance. This floor is the first hard number in the category's eligibility test.

The rule offers two separate routes past the bar. One is a run of individual releases that reaches five singles or tracks. The other is a single album. An act needs to meet only one of the two.

The two routes suit different release habits. An artist who puts out songs one at a time can clear the floor without ever releasing an album. An artist whose first project is a full album clears it with that single release.

The floor measures recorded output, not reach. An artist with fewer than five singles or tracks and no album falls below it. A large audience for those few releases does not change the count.

The minimum works as a gate rather than a qualification on its own. Clearing it does not make an artist a contender. The Academy also requires a breakthrough into the public consciousness and an impact on the musical landscape during the eligibility year. That part is assessed by judgment rather than by a chart or sales threshold.

Because the floor is defined by a count, it can be checked directly against an artist's discography. The breakthrough test offers no equivalent figure to check. Whether an entry meets the rules, and where it is placed among the categories, is decided through the Academy's review process.

The minimum is not tied to an artist's first release or first year of making music. An act that crossed the five-track or one-album line years earlier is not excluded for that reason alone. A separate ceiling on total output sets the limit at the other end of the scale.

The Recording Academy's guidance sets a ceiling for Best New Artist. A candidate can have released no more than 30 singles or tracks, or three albums. An act whose catalog has grown beyond that point falls outside the category's release limits.

The cap is one side of a two-sided release test. A separate minimum sets the floor. Between the two lies a band of output, and an act has to sit inside it before the Academy weighs anything else.

The ceiling sits higher than the word "new" suggests. Three albums means more than one complete record already in circulation. Thirty tracks is more material than a single album normally holds. An artist close to the limit already has a substantial body of recorded work.

The cap counts releases, not years. It measures how much music an artist has put out, not how long the artist has been active. A musician who records slowly can spend a long time in the industry and still stay under the limit. That is one reason a performer with a long career can still appear in the category.

Staying under the ceiling is necessary but not sufficient. The Academy also looks for a breakthrough and an impact on the musical landscape during the eligibility year. Both are assessed by judgment. The Academy's review process decides whether an entry is eligible and where it belongs.

The 2025 field shows how far the category can reach into established careers. Sabrina Carpenter was a nominee at the 2025 ceremony after releasing several albums before that year. Her place in the field shows that a sizable recording history does not by itself end an artist's claim to being new in the Academy's sense.

For anyone trying to predict a Best New Artist field, the cap answers one narrow question. It rules out acts whose output has already passed the limit. It says nothing about whether a qualifying act has made enough of a mark to earn a nomination. That second question is left to the Academy's judgment.

The heart of Grammy Best New Artist eligibility is a judgment call. The Recording Academy's guidance asks whether a candidate achieved a "breakthrough into the public consciousness" and made an impact on the musical landscape during the eligibility year.

That wording carries most of the weight in the category. The release limits on either side of it are fixed counts. The breakthrough test is phrased in words instead.

The Academy does not tie the test to a chart position or a sales figure. No commercial benchmark settles the question automatically.

That gap is the main source of confusion around the category. The word "new" suggests a statement about when an artist started. The Academy's language points elsewhere. It asks when the public began paying attention, and whether that moment landed inside the eligibility year.

Two phrases in the guidance do separate work. "Public consciousness" reads as a question of awareness: whether listeners beyond an existing following came to know the artist. "Impact on the musical landscape" reads as a question of influence on music itself during that period.

A qualitative standard has to be applied case by case. The Academy's review process handles that task, deciding which acts are eligible and where submissions are placed. Each eligibility year brings a new set of candidates to weigh against the same wording.

Timing matters as much as the breakthrough itself. A rise that lands before or after the eligibility year does not meet the test as written. That makes the date of an artist's breakthrough, rather than the date of a first release, the deciding question.

The release limits work differently. Whether an act falls inside them can be settled by counting its singles, tracks and albums. The breakthrough test adds a second layer that only the Academy's reading of an artist's year can resolve.

In practice, eligibility for this award cannot be checked against a public scoreboard.

A long career does not rule an artist out of Best New Artist. The Recording Academy does not tie eligibility to a first release or to the year an act started making music. What matters is whether the artist's breakthrough falls within the eligibility period.

The category's name suggests otherwise. That gap between the label and the rule is where much of the confusion starts. In everyday speech, "new" implies a debut. At the Grammys, it marks the moment an artist reaches a wide audience, not the moment that artist first put music out.

Two separate timelines are in play. One is the career itself: the first recordings, the early releases and the years of building a following. The other is the breakthrough, when the artist moves into the public consciousness and makes an impact on the musical landscape. Only the second timeline decides whether an act counts as new.

An artist can spend years releasing music to a modest audience and still become eligible later. If the wider breakthrough arrives during the eligibility period, the earlier work does not cancel it out. The length of a career is not a mark against an act by itself.

A long history still has limits. The Academy's guidance caps the number of singles, tracks and albums an eligible artist can have released. A veteran act qualifies only if its catalog stays under those caps and its breakthrough meets the Academy's standard.

The decision does not rest on an artist's own claim to be new. The Academy's review process determines which entries are eligible and where they are placed. That review, rather than a fixed debut date, settles whether an act with an earlier history moves forward.

Best New Artist belongs to the General Field, alongside Record of the Year, Album of the Year and Song of the Year. An act with a long career that clears the test competes in that same field as a new artist.

For listeners, the useful question is about timing rather than biography. An artist with years of releases behind them can still be a valid Best New Artist contender. The test is when the public caught on, not when the music began.

Chappell Roan won Best New Artist at the Grammy ceremony held in February 2025. Her debut single, "Good Hurt," had come out in 2017, eight years before that win.

Her record shows how far the Recording Academy's idea of "new" can stretch. By the time she collected the award, she had been releasing music for most of a decade. The category did not treat that history as a barrier.

Her timeline has three fixed points. The first is "Good Hurt" in 2017. The second is her debut album, The Rise and Fall of a Midwest Princess, which came out in 2023. The third is the February 2025 ceremony.

The spacing between those points matters. Six years separated the debut single from the debut album. Another two years separated the album from the win. A reader who looks only at the first date would expect a veteran act, not a new one.

The album also settles one side of the numerical test. Under the Academy's guidance, a single album is enough on its own to meet the minimum release requirement. A first album also leaves room below the ceiling the guidance sets on albums.

Meeting the release limits does not finish the assessment. The Academy also asks whether a candidate broke into the public consciousness and made an impact on the musical landscape during the eligibility year. That part of the test rests on judgment rather than a chart position or a sales total.

Her win shows how the two parts of the test interact. The album answered the numerical question. The judgment question turned on the period the award covered, not on the age of her career.

For anyone looking at future Best New Artist fields, her timeline offers a practical marker. A debut single several years before a nomination does not rule an artist out. An earlier album does not rule an artist out either, provided the catalog stays under the release cap.

Sabrina Carpenter was one of eight Best New Artist nominees at the 2025 ceremony, even though she had released several albums before that year. Her nomination shows that an existing catalog does not, by itself, keep an artist out of the category.

The full 2025 field was Benson Boone, Sabrina Carpenter, Chappell Roan, Doechii, Khruangbin, Raye, Shaboozey and Teddy Swims. Roan took the award.

The list put artists at different points in their careers on the same ballot. That mix is built into how the category works.

Carpenter's recorded output makes the point clear. The category's name suggests a debut act. Her discography pointed the other way, with several albums released before the ceremony that recognized her.

What put her in the running was the timing of her breakthrough, not the age of her career. The question was whether her wider public impact fell inside the period the 2025 ceremony covered. Her nomination reflects a judgment that it did.

The same lens applied to the rest of the field. No nominee was measured against a fixed number of years in the industry. Each was assessed on whether a breakthrough landed within the period the ceremony covered.

That is how a longer catalog and a shorter one could share a ballot under the same rules.

The 2025 list also offers a practical guide for reading future nominations. A familiar name with past albums is not automatically ruled out. A newer name is not automatically ruled in. Each case turns on when the artist's wider audience arrived, and on the Academy's view of that timing.

Jazz bassist and singer Esperanza Spalding won Best New Artist at the 2011 Grammy ceremony. The other nominees that year were Justin Bieber, Drake, Florence + the Machine and Mumford & Sons.

The result is often cited as evidence that the category does not follow popularity alone. That reading rests on the contrast between the winner and the rest of the field.

Every act on that list had cleared the same eligibility screen before the vote. The Recording Academy's review process decides who can compete in the category. Its guidance sets one minimum and one maximum for released music, and those limits apply to every candidate regardless of genre. Spalding, Bieber, Drake, Florence + the Machine and Mumford & Sons all fell inside those bounds.

Meeting those limits only puts an artist in contention. The deciding standard is whether the artist achieved a breakthrough into the public consciousness during the eligibility year. The Academy also looks for an impact on the musical landscape in that same period.

Neither standard is measured by a chart position or a sales total. Both require judgment. The same body of work can be read in more than one way. That leaves room for an outcome that differs from a ranking by audience size.

Best New Artist belongs to the Grammys' General Field, which is not tied to any one genre. Genre categories elsewhere in the Grammys group artists by musical style. Best New Artist does not.

That open format put Spalding on the same ballot as Bieber and Drake in 2011. A jazz bassist, a pop singer and a rapper were judged against one another under a single standard. Two bands completed the field.

The 2011 result also shows why the category is hard to forecast from fan bases alone. Voters weigh breakthrough and impact, not only popularity. Their verdict can differ from the one the charts would suggest.

Chance the Rapper won Best New Artist at the 2017 ceremony. He built his audience by giving his mixtapes away for free and reaching listeners through streaming.

His case shows the breakthrough standard applied to an artist who did not sell his music in the usual way. The Recording Academy asks whether a candidate broke into the public consciousness during the eligibility year. It also asks whether the artist made an impact on the musical landscape in that same period.

Neither question is framed in terms of revenue. The Academy answers both by judgment, without a chart position or sales total as a cutoff. That left room for an artist whose listeners found him through free downloads and streams rather than purchases. A free release can still reach a large audience, and the test looks at reach and impact, not at money changing hands.

The free-release route also raises a practical question about the numerical rules. Best New Artist candidates must clear a minimum release count and stay under a ceiling. How a free mixtape fits that count is a matter for the Academy's review process, which settles eligibility and category placement.

His night produced more than one trophy. Chance the Rapper also won Best Rap Album at the same ceremony. That paired a win in a genre category with a win in the General Field, the group that holds Best New Artist.

The two awards measured different things. Best Rap Album honored a body of recorded work within one genre. Best New Artist honored the arrival of the artist across all of music.

His win also separates two ideas that the category's name tends to blur. Putting out music is one event, and breaking through is another. Chance the Rapper had released free projects before the ceremony that honored him. The award marked when his audience arrived, not when his first release did.

Milli Vanilli won Best New Artist in 1990 and lost the award later that same year. The Recording Academy revoked it in November 1990 after it emerged that the duo had not sung on their album.

It remains the best-known rescinded award in the category.

Most arguments about Best New Artist concern timing. Listeners ask whether an act's catalog is too large, or whether a breakthrough happened in the right year. Others ask whether the breakthrough was real at all. The Milli Vanilli case raised a different question. It asked whether the winners had performed the vocals on the record that made them famous.

The eligibility rules do not settle that question. Release limits measure how much music an act has put out. The breakthrough standard measures public impact during the eligibility year. Neither test, by itself, confirms who sang on a recording.

By winning, the duo had cleared the breakthrough standard. The problem was what that rise rested on. The album behind it carried vocals the duo had not sung.

The timing of the decision sets the case apart. The revocation did not come before the ceremony. It came after voting had ended and the award had been presented. That sequence shows a Best New Artist win can be reopened after the fact.

The case also clarifies what the category honors. Best New Artist goes to an artist, not to a single record or song. An act's claim to be new rests on its own work reaching the public. The 1990 revocation tested that link between the credited act and the music.

The case marks an outer boundary for the category. Most debates over who counts as new turn on judgment about timing and impact. The Milli Vanilli decision turned on a fact about who performed the music. The Academy treated that fact as grounds to take the award back.

Billie Eilish won Best New Artist at the 2020 ceremony, and Olivia Rodrigo won it at the 2022 ceremony. Both had released music earlier. In each case, the breakthrough came in the year the award covered.

Their wins came at separate ceremonies, but they follow the same logic. For both, the Recording Academy looked past an artist's first release. It focused on the point when her music broke through.

The category does not ask when someone started recording. It asks when the wider public started paying attention. It also asks whether that attention changed the musical landscape during the eligibility year.

Eilish and Rodrigo each had a recording history before the year that mattered. That history did not count against them. Songs released ahead of a breakthrough can stay in an artist's catalog without closing off the award, as long as the total stays within the Academy's release ceiling.

Earlier releases can also work in a candidate's favor. The Academy sets a minimum amount of released music for consideration. An artist who puts out songs for some time before a breakout is likely to have cleared that floor by the time the public notices.

The harder question is timing. The Academy judges a breakthrough into the public consciousness rather than measuring it with a chart position or sales total. For Eilish and Rodrigo, that call tied eligibility to the moment of breakthrough, not to the year each first released music.

Ceremony dates add a second layer of confusion. The year attached to a Grammy win refers to the ceremony, not to the release period the award covers. Anyone comparing a winner's history with the rules should match the breakthrough to the eligibility period, not to the date of the show. For Eilish and Rodrigo, that means reading each win against the eligibility year it covered, not the ceremony year attached to the win.

Bosses can set strict office schedules if they respect staff's family time

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Many leaders decide alone which days staff come into the office. Fewer people quit when managers also ask when their workday should end

A version of this article originally appeared in Quartz’s Leadership newsletter. Sign up here to get the latest leadership news and insights straight to your inbox.

Some of the country's largest companies have spent the past few years ordering people back to their desks. At the end of 2024, Amazon $AMZN's chief executive, Andy Jassy, told corporate staff to come in five days a week. In the throes of the COVID-19 pandemic, Goldman Sachs $GS CEO David Solomon said working from home was "an aberration that we're going to correct as soon as possible." Many workers protested. But both men were determined to have employees working the same way they had before the pandemic.

Millions of office workers spent more than a year working from home. Many of them stopped working at the same hour every day and stopped answering messages late at night. Many more found meaning in their jobs. Once they were ordered back to the office, though, they had to decide whether they could maintain the same schedule without looking like they were slacking off.

Employees put up with return-to-office mandates if their manager accepts the schedule they built at home, according to Harvard Business School's Grace Mele-Cormier and colleagues. The problem is less about the time they have to come in and more about settling what time people can leave.

Mele-Cormier and his team found that employees tend to quit less often and earn slightly higher performance reviews when they're allowed to maintain the same working schedules as before. But many employees still fear that their boss will think they care less about the job if they go home at the same hour every day. They become less tense once they know their manager is on board, according to HBR's researchers.

The problem is that many managers never tell employees which hours they're expected to work. Their silence on the topic forces staff to assume they have to continue answering emails and messages at all hours of the day and night, just as they had done before the pandemic. Some employees end up leaving their roles for ones that have a clearer schedule.

Executives see the resignations, but they rarely learn that employees quit over their working hours. To executives, some teams seem to lose people for no reason while other teams don't.

Many managers never ask employees what time they need to leave each day. They assume people coming back to the office want one fixed schedule from their boss, with the same start and end time for everyone. If a manager asks each person what hours they need, the team ends up with different schedules for different people, and many managers worry they'll lose control of the team's day.

They also fear their staff will think the boss has no plan for how the team should work. Asking, though, actually leaves a manager with a better plan, according to Dan Cable, a London Business School professor. By hearing employees out, managers can spot the people most likely to quit over their working hours while there is still time to change their schedules. But Cable warns against giving employees decision-making powers. "You get a voice, not a vote," Cable told an audience at the school. "Listening shapes better decisions. It does not remove the leader's responsibility to decide."

Acting on what employees say doesn't always mean giving them what they asked for. That applies in almost every situation. After Jennifer Dulski and her leadership team laid off employees at Change.org, Dulski, then its president and chief operating officer, met with the remaining employees in several countries, according to the Society for Human Resource Management. She couldn't bring back the jobs the company cut. So her team started showing employees the company's finances instead. Once employees saw the numbers, Dulski said, they understood why their leaders had cut staff.

Other leaders change a plan or cover a cost after employees speak up. Geno Cutolo spent his first 90 days as president of staffing firm Adecco North America meeting employees across the business. He then redesigned a reorganization Adecco had already planned, to try to remove many of the frustrations and obstacles employees described to him. "It's important to act on what you hear," Cutolo said.

At Safeguard Global, the HR team heard that European employees were worried about winter heating bills, so the company paid them a monthly stipend through the winter, chief people officer Katherine Loranger said. In each case, employees could point to something that changed because they spoke up.

Employees expect the same proof after a manager asks about their hours. A manager can approve working hours, move team meetings to times everyone can make, or explain why a schedule can't change. If a manager asks and then changes and explains nothing, employees conclude the manager never had any intention to listen in the first place. Employees then stop telling managers what they need to know, and managers can't possibly tell which employees are thinking about leaving until they hand in their notice.

U.S. 8th Graders Gradually Lost Equivalent of a Year's Learning Since 2013; New Research Implies In-School Cell Phone Use May Account for Half of the Historical Loss

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Strict Cell Phone Bans Using Lockable Pouches Restore 1â2 Weeks of Student Learning Each Year, Harvard Study Shows

First National Analysis of Student-Level Data: Follows 3.3 Million Individual Students From 4,600 Schools

CAMBRIDGE, Mass., Nov. 17, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Today, Dr. Tom Kane, Faculty Director of the Center for Education Policy Research (CEPR) at Harvard University, in partnership with researchers at NWEA and Dartmouth, released findings from the first national analysis comparing achievement growth for individual students, before and after strict cell phone bans. Between 2013 and 2024, eighth-grade math and reading achievement fell by roughly one grade equivalent (a gradual loss of approximately 0.01 to 0.02 standard deviations per year), coinciding with the rapid adoption of broadband smart phones. Students were losing the equivalent of 2â3 weeks of learning every year. Although small on an annual basis, the loss accumulated.

Until recently, there has been little direct evidence on how cell phone use may have contributed to these achievement losses. By studying achievement growth after phones were banned, this study suggests that banning in-school cell phone use restores the equivalent of 1â2 weeks of lost learning each year, or about half of the annual decline in achievement since 2013.

The authors analyze data from 3.3 million students attending 4,600 schools nationally during the 2021â22 through 2025â26 school years. The study used NWEA MAP Growth data from districts across 49 states and the District of Columbia to measure math and reading growth among students in grades 3 through 10. The study measures the change in student achievement growth after schools implemented strict cell phone bans, requiring students to store their phones in lockable pouches (Yondr pouches) for the day. The study also provides the first evidence on statewide bans during the 2025â26 school year. Key findings include:

"Around 2013, U.S. students started losing 2â3 weeks of learning each year. It's been a slow leak that, over time, has put U.S. 8th graders almost a year behind. Strict cell phone bans seem to plug about half of that leak," said Tom Kane, Harvard professor and one of the leaders of the study.

"While schools should not expect a large boost immediately, storing phones is a cost-effective way to add 1â2 weeks of instruction each year. That could add up over time, just as the initial declines did," said Douglas Staiger, Professor of Economics at Dartmouth College, a study co-author.

Despite the recent progress in understanding cell phone bans, much less is known about the effect of cell phones and social media outside of school. The recent Meta settlement, in which the company has agreed to limit access for minors outside of school hours, provides a starting point for learning about the consequences of cell phone use after school and at night. To take advantage of that opportunity, we recommend the following steps:

With cooperation from the social media companies, state leaders and researchers have an opportunity not just to track the effect of the Meta settlement restrictions, but to improve them.

"We have lacked the data for understanding the role that cell phones are playing in students' lives. Obviously, the social media companies know how much time students are spending on their platforms, but their data have not been linked to student academic or non-academic outcomes. We have an opportunity to build an evidence base on any negative impacts of social media use on students and to find commonsense regulations for reducing harms," said Harvard Professor Tom Kane.

Teacher prep programs probed in Education Department’s latest anti-DEI push

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The agency said diversification efforts disregard “merit,” but critics say the programs level the playing field for historically excluded candidates.

The U.S. Department of Education opened a string of investigations into teacher preparation programs on Nov. 13, alleging that they discriminate on the basis of race under Title VI or sex under Title IX.

The investigations mark one of the first times the department's Office for Civil Rights has used civil rights protections to challenge diversification efforts in teacher scholarships, hiring, training and recruitment practices.

The programs at the center of the investigations are aimed at recruiting historically underrepresented groups in the teaching profession, such as men and teachers of color.

“Race, national origin, or sex should not decide who will shape America’s next generation, yet many states and universities seem to prioritize recruiting and training ‘minority’ candidates rather than evaluating candidates based solely on merit,” said Assistant Secretary for Civil Rights Kimberly Richey in a Nov. 13 statement.

“Education agencies and institutions that consider race, national origin, or sex as a means to withhold opportunities in selecting, supporting, and preparing teaching candidates violate federal law and compromise education excellence."

Richey concluded that students should be "judged on their hard work and merit, not prejudged by the color of their skin or their sex.”

EdTrust, a nonprofit that advocates for racial and economic equity in American education, said in a statement to K-12 Dive that the Trump administration is using civil rights protections meant to "dismantle discrimination and expand opportunity" to instead "undermine pathways into teaching for people historically excluded from the profession betrays that purpose."

“The administration’s false divide between ‘merit’ and diversity perpetuates the damaging suggestion that teachers of color are less qualified or less deserving," said Eric Duncan, director of Pre-K-12 policy of EdTrust, in the Nov. 16 statement. "It discounts their skills, expertise, and contributions while ignoring the systemic barriers that have denied talented people opportunities for generations. Scholarships that address those barriers help ensure that family wealth does not determine who can become a teacher."

The Education Department's investigations, Duncan said, "risk turning civil rights enforcement into a tool for preserving the very inequities it should confront.”

CUNY, which houses the NYC Men Teach Program being investigated by the department, said in a statement to K-12 Dive that it was "proud of its role educating the teachers of tomorrow and our programs are open to all students regardless of race, gender or national origin."

According to the city's website, the program was created around 2015 in collaboration with the federal Education Department and then-Mayor Bill de Blasio with the goal of putting an additional 1,000 men of color into the city’s teacher pipeline.

A March 2026 evaluation of the program conducted for the NYC mayor's office by Westat, which provides research services for government agencies, shows that the program contributed over 3,100 new male teachers of color to New York City Public Schools between school years 2015–16 and 2023–24.

The American Association of Colleges for Teacher Education said in a Nov. 16 email to K-12 Dive, “Strengthening and diversifying the educator pipeline is essential to addressing persistent teacher shortages and ensuring every PK-12 student has access to well-prepared, effective teachers. Decades of research confirm that a diverse educator workforce benefits all students academically, socially, and emotionally." AACTE added that it does not comment on active federal inquiries involving specific institutions or state agencies.

While the investigations are some of the first of their kind by OCR, they build on previous efforts by the Trump administration to weed out DEI in teacher preparation programs — especially in the wake of the U.S. Supreme Court’s 2023 ruling in Students for Fair Admissions v. Harvard.

Some of the earliest federal grants terminated in 2025 by the administration were under the Supporting Effective Educator Development Grant Program, the Teacher Quality Partnership Program and the Teacher and School Leader Incentive Program, due to what it said was the promotion of "divisive ideologies". The department admitted in a March court filing that it had cancelled 104 out of 109 active TQP and SEED grants.

The loss of over 100 federal teacher training grants last year caused at least 127 teacher candidates to drop out of their programs and another 259 accepted candidates to not enroll after all, according to survey findings released in October.

Those cuts were preceded by an anti-DEI directive that has since been heavily litigated and was most recently blocked in court in September.

That anti-DEI letter specifically called for agency personnel to review all new grant awards and issued grants to ensure those federal dollars “do not fund discriminatory practices — including in the form of DEI.” The directive added that “Illegal DEI policies and practices can violate both the letter and purpose of Federal civil rights law and conflict with the Department’s policy of prioritizing merit, fairness, and excellence in education.”

The Justice Department in August 2025 also warned districts they could be exposed to legal liability by asking job applicants how their “cultural background informs their teaching,” using recruitment strategies targeting candidates from specific geographic areas or racial backgrounds, and asking job candidates to describe how they overcame obstacles.

Since the Supreme Court’s decision in Students for Fair Admissions v. Harvard, civil rights groups have raised concerns that it would set back educational equity efforts even outside of race-conscious admissions. That decision undid race-conscious policies in college admissions, and the Education Department under Trump has used it to justify scaling back other diversity, equity and inclusion initiatives in education.

Scholarship availability, teacher pipelines and student affinity groups were among the top areas beyond college access that advocates were concerned could be impacted in the wake of the ruling.

World of Warcraft: Forever's in-game economy has been ravaged by an exploit that allowed players to farm thousands of gold

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

World of Warcraft: Forever has suffered its first economic nightmare, as a dungeon farm exploit has left some players with thousands of gold in their bags while the vast majority of players are barely above their first hundred.

This exploit was quickly and quietly hotfixed by Blizzard, but not before a currently-unknown number of players were able to make out like bandits. In basic terms, the new Blackmaw Hold dungeon includes several packs of non-elite, easy to kill enemies right at the start of the dungeon. Prior to the hotfix, the loot drop tables for these enemies were incorrect, and worth vastly more in terms of gold than they should have. Keen-eyed players spotted this but kept it quiet, running into the dungeon and quickly killing off these early mobs and resetting the dungeon every hour.

This was possible on every class, and according to Twitch streamer Ziqo, allowed players to farm out hundreds of gold in a few hours. He is seen streaming with over 4,000 gold - and claims other streamers made out with more than that.

For an idea of how much money that is, one of the most expensive things a player will likely buy on their character throughout the original Classic World of Warcraft is the expert riding skill. It takes a long time to save up for, and stands as a major accomplishment in the life of a character. It costs around 900 gold. These players, in just a few days of dedicated grinding, will have been able to pay this price several times over.

Looking at community hubs for the game (Reddit or the official World of Warcraft forums), regular players are demanding Blizzard take action - going further than just the already-deployed hotfix. Maya on the WoW forum wants the ill-gotten gold to be removed and for players to be banned who made use of the Blackmaw Hold exploit, writing: "Remove the gold or I and many others will just quit. This is worse than the bots at this point."

Flnrodi on Reddit, referring to streamers who took advantage of this exploit, wrote: "Not sure what to say. The fact that he did this all offstream says enough that [Ziqo] (and others) knew that this is not intended and 100 percent considered an exploit. Personally, I think there should be some kind of punishment and definitely all the gold [should be] revoked."

You may be wondering why this is such a big deal? So what if a few players can get fancy mounts faster, or snatch up some really good gear on the player auction house? Well, exorbitant sums of money in the hands of a few players can have a massive impact on a server's economy. You could, for example, use your newfound wealth to flip every listing of a certain rare resource on the auction house and mark the price up significantly for profit, which obviously inflates the price for regular players. Likewise, you can out-bid other players for rare and powerful BOE items, which in turn raises the prices of those as sellers may find they can make more cash than they could off such drops prior to the exploit.

This all has an inflationary effect, and could push a server's economy to a more expensive level faster than it otherwise would. Even if this effect isn't so drastic, these players can essentially gear out multiple characters in fantastic gear without putting in the hours, or out-bid the rest of the community on the Black Market Auction House for rare and largely unparalleled special loot.

The counter argument against punitive measures is that these players didn't cheat. They didn't buy gold from real-money-traders, nor did they plug in some external tool to give them an unfair advantage over others. They simply put in the time to get to around level 55, ran a dungeon, and killed mobs repeatedly for valuable drops. Would it have been a good thing to flag the exploit the moment they found out about it? Sure. But once other players at your level take advantage of it, and no strict ruling around whether doing so is punishable, it's arguably in a player's best interest to join in while the going is good.

As of writing, there's been no official word from Blizzard on what it intends to do beyond fixing the exploit. Given the required level to take on Blackmaw Hold, the number of exploiters cannot be too large, and the sheer amount of gold they gained in a small amount of time should make them easy to flag. Rolling back their accounts and reverting the gold they gained back to a pre-exploit amount certainly appears to be a popular option. Whether Blizzard actually does so, or enforces other punishments, is the big question.

Aston Villa could reportedly be tempted to part ways with Boubacar Kamara next summer, but only if an interested club puts an enormous offer on the table.

The 26-year-old has become one of Unai Emery’s most trusted midfielders and remains a key part of Villa’s plans.

However, interest from Chelsea and other major clubs has increased in recent weeks, forcing the Midlands side to consider what level of bid might eventually make them reconsider their position.

According to Football Insider, former Aston Villa chief executive Keith Wyness believes an offer of around £80million could make the club seriously think about selling Kamara.

Villa are not actively looking to move the France international on, and his importance has only increased this season. Kamara has started every Premier League game so far and leads Villa for defensive contributions per 90 minutes.

Chelsea have emerged as one of the clubs most interested in the midfielder, with their recruitment team assessing whether a future deal could be possible.

Villa remain in a strong negotiating position, though. Kamara is under contract until 2030, meaning they have no need to accept anything below a premium fee.

Kamara is not simply a defensive midfielder who breaks up play. He is comfortable receiving the ball under pressure, reads danger exceptionally well and gives Emery’s side balance when they commit players forward.

A CaughtOffside report recently noted that Villa are monitoring Chelsea midfielder Romeo Lavia, while also highlighting growing interest in Kamara from Inter Milan, Arsenal and Liverpool.

That wider interest strengthens Villa’s hand. The bigger issue is squad depth. With Amadou Onana sidelined long term, losing Kamara would leave a major hole in midfield and force Villa straight back into the market.

For that reason, a January sale should be considered almost impossible.

Next summer could be different if Chelsea or another club arrives with a huge offer, but Villa should only entertain a deal if the money is significant enough to fund a genuine replacement and strengthen other areas.

Kamara is too important to sell cheaply. If someone wants him, £80m feels like the right starting point.

The Homeownership Gap Between Young and Old Americans Has Doubled—These Cities Reveal Why

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The homeownership gap between younger and older Americans has more than doubled over the past four decades, with some of the nation's most expensive cities seeing the sharpest increases, according to a new report.

The analysis, from the Urban Institute, finds that nationally just 43% of households headed by adults aged 25 to 35 owned their homes in 2024, compared with 79% of those headed by adults 65 and older. The 35.8-percentage-point difference is more than twice the 17.5-point gap recorded in 1980.

But that divide pales in comparison to the gaping chasm in some of America's most expensive metropolitan areas. Between 1980 and 2024, the homeownership gap grew by 29 percentage points in Denver, 23 points in New York City, and 22 points in San Francisco, compared with 18.3 points nationally.

But those widening gaps also expose a deeper economic trade-off: The cities offering young Americans the greatest opportunities to earn more may be the very places preventing them from building wealth through homeownership

"The cities with the strongest labor markets tend to be the least affordable, so some will take the better job and rent indefinitely, giving up equity accumulation," explains Hannah Jones, senior economist at Realtor.com®. "Others will pass up higher-paying roles or move to cheaper, lower-opportunity markets in order to buy."

In 1980, younger households in Denver were nearly as likely as their older counterparts to own a home.

At the time, 55% of households headed by adults aged 25 to 35 owned their homes, compared with 67% of households headed by adults 65 and older.

By 2024, the younger group's homeownership rate had fallen to just 37%, while the older group's rate climbed to 78%, expanding the divide from 12 to 41 percentage points.

But the decline in young homeownership may reflect something besides worsening affordability: Denver's success in attracting young workers.

"Two things are likely at work. One is the affordability decline itself. The other is Denver’s growth as an economic hub that appeals to young workers, many of whom arrive as renters," says Jones. "That adds renters to the mix and dilutes the young homeownership rate, even for people who never intended to buy."

There, younger households were already far less likely to own homes in 1980, when their homeownership rate stood at 28%. By 2024, it had slipped only slightly, to 26%.

Older New Yorkers, meanwhile, saw their homeownership rate climb from approximately 43% to 63% over the same period.

"In New York, homeownership has always been expensive and out of reach for most young households, so there was little room to fall," Jones explains. "Young homeownership slipped only slightly, which suggests the current rate is close to a floor."

She suggests low COVID-19 pandemic-era mortgage rates may have helped some older households that had been waiting to purchase finally enter the market.

And regardless of how the divide developed, entering homeownership later can carry lasting financial consequences.

Separate research from Realtor.com, co-authored by Jones, found that purchasing a home by age 30 was associated with 22.5% higher net worth—approximately $119,000 more—by age 50, compared with buying in one's 40s, even after accounting for income, education, and marital status.

Those advantages can extend across generations, too. Children raised in homeowner households were 18.4 percentage points more likely to become homeowners themselves by age 35, the same research found.

One of the Urban Institute's most striking findings concerns a milestone that comes before homeownership: establishing an independent household.

In 1980, young adults in high-cost metropolitan areas were slightly more likely than their counterparts nationally to head their own households. By 2024, that relationship had reversed.

Just 41% of young adults in expensive markets were household heads, the lowest share among the study's housing cost categories.

Instead, more are living with parents, other relatives, or roommates. Research from Realtor.com backs this up, finding that a third of young adults lived with their parents across the country in 2025.

Yet expensive cities haven't lost their appeal. The share of young households living in high-cost markets actually edged up from 28% in 1980 to 31% in 2024, partly reflecting a broader shift toward urban areas.

What's changed is their housing tenure. In those markets, the homeownership rate among younger households fell from 40% to 32% over the same period.

So young adults are continuing to pursue opportunities in expensive cities, but fewer are establishing households of their own once they get there. And another finding suggests that rapidly rising prices may be changing the age composition of metropolitan areas, regardless of how expensive those places were to begin with.

Between 2014 and 2024, metropolitan areas experiencing faster home price appreciation generally saw declines in young adults' share of the population, the Urban Institute found.

The relationship held across high-, moderate-, and low-cost markets. But Jones cautions that the findings don't necessarily mean young people are being priced out.

"That can come from young people leaving, older people moving in, or both," she says. "Florida metros on the included chart may be an example. Their older populations are large and grew significantly during the pandemic, which could erode the young share on its own."

Indeed, the relationship between home prices and demographic changes could run in either direction.

"Young people may be fleeing places where costs are climbing, or older, more financially equipped buyers may be moving in and competing for homes," Jones says. "That pushes prices up, and the newcomers then make up a larger share of the population."

Determining whether younger residents are actually leaving would require examining changes in the number of young adults, not just their share of the population.

Still, other economic research suggests that housing costs can constrain where workers pursue employment.

A May 2025 analysis from the Federal Reserve Bank of Richmond found that geographic mobility has declined across all age groups since the 1990s. Among the contributing factors, researchers identified restrictions on housing supply in economically productive cities, where high costs can discourage workers from relocating to pursue better opportunities.

The researchers also identified another potential obstacle: Older homeowners have become less responsive to rising local housing costs, making them more likely to stay put even as prices climb. In supply-constrained markets, that can make it harder for newcomers to move in.

Meanwhile, a February 2026 analysis from the St. Louis Fed found that home prices had risen approximately 207% from 2000 to 2024, outpacing the roughly 155% increase in per-capita incomes.

Together, the findings suggest that the costs of entering high-opportunity housing markets could affect younger Americans' earnings prospects as well as their ability to build wealth.

There may be a way around that trade-off for some.

"Remote work may be loosening the link between where people live and where the jobs are, which could enable more households to achieve homeownership without giving up job opportunities," Jones says.

But that flexibility is not available to everyone. Workers whose jobs require them to be physically present may face a more difficult choice between buying a home where they can afford one and pursuing careers in the cities where their skills command the highest wages.

And unlike the wealth accumulated by longtime homeowners, the career opportunities they pass up may be difficult to recover later.

Allaire Conte is a senior advice writer covering real estate and personal finance trends. She previously served as deputy editor of home services at CNN Underscored Money and was a lead writer at Orchard, where she simplified complex real estate topics for everyday readers. She holds an MFA in Nonfiction Writing from Columbia University and a BFA in Writing, Literature, and Publishing from Emerson College. When she’s not writing about homeownership hurdles and housing market shifts, she’s biking around Brooklyn or baking cakes for her friends.

South Korea will ground all planes nationwide for 35 minutes on Thursday so students can hear the English listening section of the country’s high-stakes college entrance exam, known as Suneung, in silence. No aircraft will take off or land between 1:05 pm and 1:40 pm local time, or 9:35 am to 10:10 am IST, the transport ministry said. The pause will affect 130 flights, including 81 international and 49 domestic services. Planes already flying must stay at least 3 kilometres above ground, with only emergency flights exempt. Helicopters, light aircraft and drones are also covered, and anyone breaking the drone ban could face criminal charges. Airlines will contact passengers about new schedules in advance. The exam also affects finance. The Korea Exchange will open an hour later than usual at 10 am and close at 4:30 pm, giving students and parents clearer roads during the morning rush.

Brown Advisory Sustainable Small-Cap Core Strategy Q3 2026 Commentary
